Marstacimab-hncq
HYMPAVZI
Anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor
PF-06741086
Pfizer Inc.
INJECTION
The recommended dosing regimen comprise of a loading dose
of 300 mg for the first week followed by once weekly dosing of
150 mg by subcutaneous injection. The recommended dosage
of HYMPAVZI for patients 12 years of age and older, weighing
at least 35 kg is as follows:
• Day 1: Administer 300 mg (two 150 mg injections) by
subcutaneous injection (loading dose)
• Day 8 and thereafter: Administer 150 mg every week by
subcutaneous injection
For routine prophylaxis (to prevent or reduce the frequency of
bleeding episodes) in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of
age and older with:
• Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without factor
VIII inhibitors, or
• Hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) without factor
IX inhibitors.
234442002 (Hereditary factor VIII deficiency disease without
inhibitor)
438792009 (Hereditary factor IX deficiency disease without
inhibitor)
Approval
The recommended dosage of HYMPAVZI for adult and pediatric
patients 12 years of age and older is as follows:
Loading dose
• 300 mg (two 150 mg subcutaneous injections)
• If more than one injection is required to deliver a complete
dose, administer each injection at a different injection site.
Maintenance dose
• One week after the loading dose, initiate maintenance dosing
of 150 mg every week by subcutaneous injection on the
same day each week, at any time of day.

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On October 11, 2023, Pfizer, Inc, submitted a new BLA for marstacimab for the following
indications: HYMPAVZI is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)-directed antibody indicated
for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and
pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII
deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX
deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
On May 18, 2016, marstacimab received orphan drug designation for treatment of Hemophilia A
and Hemophilia B patients with or without inhibitors. Fast Track Designation was granted on
September 17, 2019.
The Applicant submitted results from one adequate and well-controlled clinical study
(Study B7841005) that served as the equivalent of two studies, which provides substantial
evidence of effectiveness (SEE) of marstacimab for the indication. Study B7841005 is an openlabel, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment) study of marstacimab in
patients aged 12 years and above with severe HA and moderately severe to severe HB with or
without inhibitors. Participants were enrolled to independent cohorts based on presence or
absence of inhibitor and treatment regimen at the time of enrollment (on-demand or prophylaxis
factor replacement). The efficacy of marstacimab was based on the results from the noninhibitor
cohort, observational phase on-demand (OP-OD), comprising participants who received factor
on demand prior to enrollment and during the 6-month OP and cohort observational phase
prophylaxis (OP-PPX), comprising participants who received prophylaxis with factor prior to
enrollment and during the 6-month observation period. Following the OP, all participants
received 12 months of marstacimab prophylaxis during the active treatment phase (ATP). For
each cohort, the study compared treatment with factor-replacement therapy during OP with that
of marstacimab prophylaxis treatment during ATP.
Marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrated superiority over on-demand factor replacement (Cohort
OP-OD) and noninferiority to prophylaxis with factor (Cohort OP-PPX) based on the annualized
bleeding rate (ABR) of targeted bleeds (primary endpoint), spontaneous, joint, total, and target
joint bleeds (secondary endpoints), in participants with HA and HB without inhibitor. An ABR
endpoint has been used to support approval for products to treat bleeding events due to
hemophilia in the past. Prevention of bleeding is considered of clinical benefit, by reducing the
morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and stroke.
The safety profile of marstacimab prophylaxis was determined to be acceptable for the proposed
indication, and toxicities can be addressed in labeling. Enhanced pharmacovigilance is
recommended for thromboembolic events based on the mechanism of action of marstacimab and
the small size of the clinical studies.

This application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary team. Clinical, clinical pharmacology,
statistical, nonclinical, and product quality review teams recommended approval of marstacimab
in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A or B without
inhibitor, and in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA without
factor and HB without factor IX inhibitors.
The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Table 2)
below. For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed
reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.

Conclusions and Reasons
Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations Hemophilia A (congentital factor VIII deficiency)
without inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital
in the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the
clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5000 live male factor IX deficiency) without inhibitors are serious
chronic diseases with significant morbidity and
births. Patients are typically diagnosed in infancy. Patients are at
morality due to bleeding.
increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening
spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations include
mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds,
hemarthrosis, and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma.
Bleeding can also occur in vital organs (including the brain if left
untreated), recurrent bleeding can lead to damage in skeletal muscles
and joints, causing progressive and debilitating arthropathy, resulting joint
dysfunction, and decreased quality of life. Adults commonly experience
hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated
with significant disease morbidity.
Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations
in the FIX gene, which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the
clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male
births. Deficient factor IX levels result in an increased risk for minor and
serious bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic
bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA described in
the text above.
One complication of HA and HB is the development of inhibitors to
factors, which is a risk associated with the use of factor replacement.
Approximately 30%-35% of patients with HA and 5%-15% of patients with
HB develop inhibitors. Patients with inhibitors often experience increased
disease morbidity and mortality because the bleeding is more difficult to
prevent or treat.

Conclusions and Reasons

Risk and risk
management

Overall Safety Findings
• The review of safety focused Study B7841005 and events that occurred
during the ATP while participants were receiving marstacimab. Data from
the two cohorts were pooled and the safety population comprised 116
adolescent and adult participants with HA or HB without inhibitors who
received at least one dose of open-label marstacimab prophylaxis during
the ATP period.
• The key safety findings were:
– There were no fatal adverse events.
– Serious adverse events (SAEs) occurred in 6% of participants;
however, only one of these SAEs was assessed as related to
marstacimab, peripheral swelling of the right calf.
– Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of marstacimab
occurred in only 2 (2%) participants. One participant discontinued
treatment due to atypical meningioma, assessed as not related, and
another discontinued due to peripheral swelling, assessed as related.
– Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were reported in 64%
of participants while receiving marstacimab prophylaxis during the
ATP. The most common TEAEs, occurring in ≥5% of participants,
were viral infection (21%), local administration reactions (10%),
hemorrhage (10%), nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and
pruritus (7%). The most frequently reported adverse reaction
observed in ≥10% of participants while receiving marstacimab with
marstacimab were injection site reactions.
• Thromboembolic events are an adverse event of special interest based
on the mechanism of action of marstacimab.
– In Study B7841005, there were no events of thrombosis reported in
participant while receiving marstacimab during the ATP.
– There was 1 case of a thromboembolic event (pulmonary embolism
and DVT) reported in a healthy participant who received marstacimab
on a clinical study.

The safety data submitted were sufficient to
characterize the toxicity profile of marstacimab.
Although all data was uncontrolled, this was
acceptable given the rarity of the disease, clear
mechanism of action, and experience with other
hemophilia drugs.

Overall the marstacimab was well tolerated.
The risks of marstacimab are generally comparable
to those of other anti-hemophilic treatments used for
prophylactic treatment for patients with hemophilia.
The identified risk of hypersensitivity and the class
risk of thromoembolism should be included in the
Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI.
Although there were no reports of thromboembolic
events in the pivotal study, given the mechanism of
action of marstacimab and the limited size of the
safety database, the review team recomments
enhanced pharmacovigilance for the risk of
thromboembolic events.

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
BLA 761369, marstacimab, provided substantial evidence of effectiveness from one adequate
and well-controlled study with two cohorts that serves as the equivalent of two studies. The
benefit-risk profile is favorable, and all disciplines recommend approval. Hemophilia (A or B)
are rare and serious diseases with limited treatment options depending upon the subtype. If left
untreated, hemophilia will result in significant morbidity and early mortality from uncontrolled
bleeding arising spontaneously, following injury, or related to surgery. In general, affected
patients start on a life-long treatment program of blood products or recombinant factor
concentrates designed to “bypass” the absent clotting factor, but this treatment carries the burden
of frequent intravenous (IV) administration, risks of infection and thrombosis, and risk for
development of inhibitors which can result in decreased or ineffective treatment. Additional
available therapy includes a nonfactor therapy, emicizumab, to prevent severe bleeding for
patients with HA with and without inhibitors.
Marstacimab treatment provides a prophylaxis treatment for patients with congenital hemophilia
A or B without inhibitors. For the subgroup of congenital HB without inhibitors, this product has
the potential to address an unmet medical need because there is no available nonfactor
replacement therapy for this subgroup. The nonclinical, clinical, statistical, and clinical
pharmacology teams support the approval based on the nonclinical and clinical data packages
and did not identify any deficiencies. No important differences of opinion were voiced during the
review process.
The clinical data package consists of a single study with two cohorts that served as two adequate
and well-controlled studies that demonstrated an effect on ABR, an endpoint that has been used
for the approval of other monoclonal antibodies or blood products for the treatment of
hemophilia. The prevention of bleeding, a clinical benefit, is expected to reduce the morbidity
and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and stroke.
Study B7851005 is an open-label, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment)
study of marstacimab in participants aged 12 years and older with severe HA and moderately
severe to severe HB, with or without inhibitors. The study was designed to allow comparison of
ABRs during the 6-month observation period, during which participants received the same
treatment regimen they were receiving at the time of enrollment, on-demand or prophylaxis with
factor, with ABRs during the ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis. The two noninhibitor cohorts
formed the basis of establishing SEE in this application.
In Cohort OP-OD, the effectiveness of marstacimab was established by demonstrating
superiority of ABR during prophylaxis with marstacimab over on-demand treatment with factor.
The mean ABR of treated bleeding for marstacimab prophylaxis was 3.18 (95% CI: 2.09, 4.85)
bleeds compared to 38 (95% CI: 31.03, 46.54) bleeds for the on-demand factor.
In Cohort OP-PPX, the effectiveness of marstacimab was established by demonstrating
noninferiority of ABR during prophylaxis with marstacimab compared to prophylaxis with
factor. Mean ABR of treated bleeds for marstacimab prophylaxis was 5.08 (3.4, 6.77) compared
to 7.85 (5.09, 10.61) for prophylaxis with factor.
10
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
The risks of marstacimab can adequately be described in labeling. Injection site reactions, based
on experience in clinical studies of marstacimab, and thromboembolic events, based on
mechanism of action and risks associated with other drugs in the class, will be included in
warnings and precautions of the United States Prescribing Information (USPI).
Because of the limited size of the safety database for marstacimab, enhanced pharmacovigilance
will be instituted for thromboembolic events.

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Marstacimab (proposed proprietary name, HYMPAVZI), a first-in-class, new molecular entity, is
a monoclonal antibody (mAb) antagonist of endogenous tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI).
TFPI is the primary inhibitor of the extrinsic coagulation cascade. TFPI initially binds to and
inhibits the factor Xa active site via its second Kunitz inhibitor domain (K2). Thus, neutralizing
the activity of TFPI may serve to enhance the extrinsic pathway and reduce or eliminate the need
for replacement factor VIII (FVIII) and factor IX (FIX). Marstacimab acts independently from
FVIII and FIX, and thus would not be expected to result in the formation of factor neutralizing
antibodies.
The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the
frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with
hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors, or
hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
The proposed dosing regimen is a loading dose of 300 mg administered by subcutaneous (SC)
injection on Day 1 followed by maintenance dosing starting one week later (Day 8) of 150 mg
every week by SC injection. The dose may be adjusted to 300 mg SC injection weekly in patients
weighing ≥50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the healthcare
provider.
Disease Background
Congenital hemophilia is a rare X-linked bleeding disorder caused by deficiencies of FVIII in
HA and FIX in HB. Patients with hemophilia can have spontaneous bleeding events and bleeding
events to even minor trauma, which can affect critical organs and be fatal. Clinical hallmarks of
hemophilia are joint and muscle hemorrhages, easy bruising, and prolonged and potentially fatal
hemorrhage after trauma or surgery. The severity of hemophilia is correlated with residual factor
levels, mild (5- <40% FVIII or FIX activity), moderate (1 to 5% FVIII or FIX activity) and
severe (<1% FVIII or FIX activity) (Srivastava et al. 2013). Approximately a third of cases of
hemophilia are severe.
The frequency of HA is approximately 17.1 cases in 100,000 newborn males (Soucie et al. 2020)
and approximately 3.3 cases in 100,000 newborn males for HB (Zimmerman and Valentino
2013), with similar estimated incidence in the United States. In HA, the absence of functional
FVIII interrupts the coagulation cascade and the ability to form a stable clot in response to
trauma. The resultant bleeding complications can be severe and life threatening and can lead to
long-term morbidity from joint damage due to recurrent bleeds. In 30 to 35% of patients with
HA, a neutralizing antibody, known as a FVIII inhibitor, forms, rendering exogenous and
endogenous FVIII nonfunctioning. Patients with FVIII inhibitors are more difficult to treat in
response to bleeding events, leading to a higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent
joint bleeds and increased risk of death from uncontrolled bleeding. In HB, bleeding tendency is
12
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
in accordance with severity of factor deficiency, and patients with severe form (FIX <1%), who
account for approximately 30 to 45% of persons with HB, usually suffer from recurrent joint,
soft-tissue and muscle bleeding. The reported rate of FIX inhibitors is 1 to 15% of all patients
with HB (Male et al. 2021).
Patients with HA and HB are typically treated with blood/recombinant factor concentrates which
supply the necessary missing clotting factors. The goal of treatment in hemophilia is to prevent
or minimize the occurrence of bleeding to improve joint function, alleviate pain, and help the
patient to continue/resume regular daily activities. However, up to 32% of patients with severe
HA and 5% of patients with severe HB develop neutralizing antibodies to the administered
factors rendering the factor concentrates ineffective to prevent bleeding events. The patients who
have hemophilia and inhibitors are then treated with a bypassing strategy designed to overcome
the missing factor and inhibitor.
Treatment with replacement factor can either be in response to a bleeding event (episodic or “on
demand”) or scheduled to prevent bleeds by maintaining factor levels in a protective range
(prophylactic treatment). The aim of prophylaxis is to reduce FVIII or FIX deficiencies and
reduced spontaneous bleeding events by maintaining factor activity of at least 1% (Berntorp and
Shapiro 2012). There is significant evidence that prophylactic treatment in children with severe
hemophilia helps prevent bleeding episodes and the associated joint damage that is a major
morbidity in patients with hemophilia (Manco-Johnson et al. 2007). In adults with severe
hemophilia, an early start of treatment with long-term prophylaxis is reported to reduce bleeding
frequency and subsequent chronic joint disease and result in better quality of life (Khawaji et al.
2011). The choice of regimens (i.e., on-demand or prophylaxis with factor) is multi-factorial and
may be based on frequency and severity of bleeding, patient preference and lifestyle, and
regional/geographic standard of care. In resource-rich countries, prophylactic treatment initiated
prior to any joint bleeds is preferred.
Over the past 50 years, the treatment options for hemophilia have advanced. Initial outcomes
measures were focused on raising the factor activity levels. With the incorporation of
prophylactic therapy, annualized bleeding rate (ABR) became the primary outcome measure
(Konkle et al. 2019). Bleeding rates can be impacted by several patient-specific factors as well as
external factors, such as clotting factor level, age, overall health, activity level, and seasonality.
There can be some subjectivity with measurement of ABR as the patient and treating physician
must each define what constitutes a bleeding event; ABR generally only measures recognized
bleeding events and may not capture subclinical bleeds. Overall, ABR is a clinically meaningful
endpoint as a reduction in bleeding events is important to patients by decreasing risk of
mortality, organ and tissue damage, including joint damage, which is associated with recurrent
bleeds, and allowing greater activity that might otherwise be limited due to risk of trauma and
bleeding.

13
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Current Therapy Options
The current treatment options for patients with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors include:
(1) Replacement of missing FVIII or FIX can be used as an episodic intravenous (IV) infusion of
the missing factor (FVIII or FIX) in response to symptoms of a bleed “on-demand” or
scheduled routine prophylactic FVIII or FIX administration to prevent bleeds from occurring.
Prophylactic treatment with replacement factors VIII or IX has proven to reduce the
frequency of bleeding events; however, it requires lifelong IV infusion every 1 to 2 weeks
resulting in treatment burden, incomplete adherence, and complications associated with
indwelling catheters (i.e., infection, thrombosis). There are two type of replacement factors
therapy:
(a) plasma-derived factors concentrate which have short half 1 to 2 days and require frequent
IV injections at based on body-mass–dependent doses.
(b) recombinant factor concentrates which have longer half-life of 1 to 2 weeks.
(2) Hemlibra (emicizumab-kxwh) SC injection is a bispecific FIXa-and factor X (FX)-directed
antibody, which was approved by FDA on November 16, 2017, for routine prophylaxis to
prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA
with inhibitors. Hemlibra was approved for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA without FVIII
inhibitors in 2018. The recommended loading dose of emicizumab is 3 mg/kg administered
by SC injection once weekly for the first 4 weeks, followed by a maintenance dose of
1.5 mg/kg once very week, or 3 mg/kg once every two weeks, or 6 mg/kg once every four
weeks. The emicizumab product label contains a boxed warning that states cases of
thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were reported when on average a
cumulative amount of >100U/kg/24 hours of activated prothrombin complex concentrate
(aPCC) was administered for 24 hours or more to patients receiving emicizumab prophylaxis.
The boxed warning contains the recommendation to monitor for the development of
thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events if aPCC is administered, and to
discontinue aPCC and suspend dosing of emicizumab if symptoms occur.
(3) Gene therapy: Gene therapy achieved by infusion of a recombinant, adeno-associated virus–
based vector containing the gene for the missing coagulation factor therapy (i.e., Hemgenix
for HB and ROCTAVIAN for HA).
The World Federation of Hemophilia (WFH) strongly recommends prophylactic treatment for
patients with hemophilia A or B with a severe phenotype sufficient to prevent bleeds at all times.
Prophylaxis should be individualized (adjusting prophylaxis dose/frequency or both), taking into
consideration patient bleeding phenotype, joint status, individual pharmacokinetics, and patient
self-assessment and preference (Srivastava et al. 2020).
Marstacimab is a first-in-class TFPI antagonist with a mechanism of action that is different than
the other available nonfactor product therapy and provides for an alternative nonfactor treatment
option for patients with HA and HB without inhibitors. Marstacimab is the first nonfactor
replacement therapy that will be available for patients with HB without inhibitors, which
addresses an unmet medical need.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The early phase studies, summarized in Section 14, assessed the bioequivalence,
pharmacokinetic (PK), and pharmacodynamic (PD) characteristics of different dosing regimen
and provided preliminary safety, immunogenicity, and tolerability of marstacimab.
Table 3 provides an overview of the clinical studies conducted to support the benefit-risk
assessment of marstacimab for participants with HA and HB without inhibitors. Results from
two independent cohorts of participants with HA and HA without inhibitor from the pivotal
study, Study 7841005 (Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPD) formed the basis of the assessment
of benefit. The assessment of benefit was based on a comparison of efficacy data from the active
treatment phase (ATP), during which all participants received marstacimab, and efficacy data
from the OP, during which participants received treatment based on their cohort assignment
(factor on-demand factor or factor prophylaxis).
The assessment of risk was based primarily on data derived from Study 7841005 during 12month ATP period with marstacimab prophylaxis along with analysis of safety data from longterm open-label study (Study B7841007) as well as a pooled safety set comprising all adverse
events occurring in participants with HA or HB with or without inhibitor occurred while
receiving marstacimab on a clinical study.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Marstacimab is an immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) mAb TFPI antagonist designed to prevent
bleeding by enhancing the extrinsic coagulation pathway in patients with hemophilia (A and B).
Both conditions are inherited X-linked genetic disorders that occur almost exclusively in males.
Only rare instances have been reported in females where both X chromosomes are affected or if
one X chromosome is affected and the other X chromosome is absent or nonfunctional.
19
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Patients with HA and HB are susceptible to bleeding due to deficiency in factor VIII and factor
IX, respectively (see Figure 1). Deficiencies in coagulation factor IX (HB) or factor VIII (HA) of
the intrinsic pathway can prevent thrombin formation causing downstream inhibition of the
coagulation cascade in hemophilia patients, which may result in excessive bleeding.
Figure 1. Intrinsic and Extrinsic Coagulation Pathway

Source: Modified Applicant figure
Abbreviations: TF: tissue factor; TFPI: tissue factor pathway inhibitor

Tissue factor (TF)1 is an extrinsic coagulation protein released in response to extravascular cell
damage that enhances FVII and FX, independent of intrinsic coagulation pathway. Thus,
strategies to increase TF activity might enhance coagulation in both HA and HB.
Normally, TF activity is negatively regulated by TFPI2 (shown in Figure 1 in red). The role of TF
and TFPI in the extrinsic coagulation pathway is depicted in Figure 2. TFPI is an endogenous
serine protease inhibitor containing at least three known Kunitz-type domains (K1, K2 and K3)
separated by short stretches of amino acids. Marstacimab binds to TFPI Kunitz 2 (K2) domain,
preventing TFPI from inhibiting TF.

1 TF is an integral membrane bound blood coagulation protein expressed in many cells in organs such as lungs,

brain, heart, testes, uterus, and placenta. Vascular tissue injury or disruption in endovascular activity, can trigger
TF/FVIIa/FXa complex to promote and activate the extrinsic coagulation pathway, enhancing the conversion of
prothrombin to thrombin and consequently formation of blood clot at the site of injury.
2 TFPI is an endogenous protease inhibitor produced by the endothelial cells of the vascular smooth muscle cells and
several blood cells (i.e., megakaryocytes/ platelets, monocyte) that circulates in plasma. There are two known
isomers of TFPI (TFPIα and TFPI β). TFPIα is the full-length isoform consisting of three Kunitz-type-domains K1,
K2, and K3, the latest not bearing inhibitory function. The anticoagulant action of TFPI is proposed to occur in a
two-stage process. Release of K2 binds FXa and inactivates it followed by K1 binding to TF-FVIIa complex that
prevents further activation of Factor X (Price et al. 2004).

20
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Figure 2. Schematic Presentation of the TF and TFPI and Marstacimab Site of Action

Source: Modified figure from Crawley and Lane (2008)
Abbreviations: FVIIa, factor VIIa; FX, factor X; FXa, factor Xa; TF, tissue factor; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor

As depicted in Figure 2, the extrinsic clotting pathway is normally triggered by TF in response to
tissue injury (1), which forms a complex with FVIIa and FX (2), activating (3) and releasing FXa
(4) into the surrounding tissue to (5) promote coagulation via the common coagulation pathway,
leading to increased thrombin and blood clotting at the site of injury (see Figure 1. Intrinsic and
Extrinsic Coagulation Pathway). Plasma TFPI regulates (blocks) the FVIIa/TF/FX complex (6),
inhibiting TF from promoting and activating FX. TFPI binding to the FVIIa/TF/FXa complex
occurs via its K2 and K1 domains. Marstacimab binds and inhibits plasma TFPI via the K2
domain (7), which increases the activity of TF and the pro-coagulatory function of FXa.
To establish proof of concept for marstacimab as a potential treatment for HA and HB, a series
of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies were conducted by the Applicant, as discussed in
Sections 5.1.1 to 5.1.3 below. These data demonstrated that marstacimab binds and inhibits the
combined K1K2 domains (via K2) of TFPI from humans and nonclinical species. The results of
21
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
these studies establish a therapeutic rationale for marstacimab to enhance extrinsic coagulation
pathway as a treatment for patients with hemophilia A and B.

5.1.1. In Vitro Characterization of Marstacimab-TFPI
Interaction
Marstacimab binds and inhibits human and animal TFPI. Marstacimab has high affinity to
human TFPI K2 (Kd =2.63±0.08nM) and the combined K1K2 domains (7.9±0.33nM) but does
not bind to the K1 domain of TFPI. In a cross-reactivity study, marstacimab was shown to have a
similar inhibitory effect on TFPI K1K2 domains across different species, suggesting their
relevance to evaluating the clinical utility (and safety) of marstacimab (Table 5). With no
binding to K1, the procoagulant activity of marstacimab is related solely to binding and
inhibition of TFPI’s K2 and K1K2 domains.

5.1.2. In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of
Marstacimab
The pharmacological activity of marstacimab was evaluated in a series of in vitro studies. In a
cell-free enzymatic assay, marstacimab dose-dependently inhibited the combined K1K2 domains
of TFPI to generate active FX with a half-maximal effective concentration (EC50) of 1312 ng/mL
and increased the combined activity of the FXa and FVIIa/TF/FXa with an EC50 of 1985 ng/mL.
Marstacimab’s ability to enhance clot formation was demonstrated using normal whole blood.
Marstacimab significantly reduced clotting time (accelerated clot formation) in whole blood
from various donors (Figure 3).

The effect of marstacimab on diluted prothrombin time (dPT) and activated partial
thromboplastin time (aPTT) as measures of clotting time was evaluated using human plasma
from healthy donors. As expected, marstacimab dose-dependently shortened dPT, a marker of
extrinsic and common pathway integrity but not aPTT, a marker of intrinsic and common
pathway integrity.
A summary of in vitro pharmacology studies is described in Table 6. Collectively, in vitro
studies show marstacimab is effective in inhibiting and neutralizing TFPI, increasing free FXa,
reducing clotting time in whole blood, and enhancing thrombin generation in platelet-poor
plasma.

5.1.3. Proof of Concept Animal Model Data
The pharmacological activity of marstacimab was evaluated in vivo in mouse models of HA
(FVIII-deficient) and HB (FIX-deficient). Marstacimab dose-dependently reduced blood loss in
both HA (76%) and HB (65%) mouse models relative to controls. As shown in Figure 4, blood
volume loss in the HA mouse model lasted up to 189 hrs (7.9 days) at a maximum dose of
6 mg/kg (a human equivalent dose of 360 mg based on body weight), which is similar to the
maximum recommended once weekly human dose of 300 mg. The in vivo studies in mouse
models suggest that marstacimab might increase fibrin deposition and increase platelet
accumulation in hemophilia patients. A summary of the in vivo model data is shown in Table 7.

In summary, in vitro and in vivo studies demonstrated marstacimab binds and inhibits K2
domain of the combined K1K2 domains of TFPI (Kd 3.7nM), but not the K1 domain.
Marstacimab neutralized TFPI, increased FXa, shortened clotting time, increased thrombin
generation in whole blood, and reduced blood loss in mouse models of HA and HB. The greatest
effects in animal models of HA and HB were achieved at 6 mg/kg, which approximates a 360 mg
marstacimab dose for a 60 kg patient. Collectively these studies support a plausible mechanism
of action by which marstacimab may be a potentially effective treatment of HA and HB.

Clearance
Marstacimab is cleared via linear and nonlinear mechanisms. Marstacimab
exhibited nonlinear pharmacokinetics due to target-mediated drug disposition
(TMDD) upon interaction with its target, i.e., TFPI. Once the target becomes
saturated, linear pathway (i.e., catabolism) dominates.
Half-life
Based on population PK analysis, median time for 50% of marstacimab to be
eliminated is approximately 7 and 10 days and median time for 90% of
marstacimab is eliminated 14 and 22 days, for 150 mg and 300 mg dose,
respectively. A typical elimination half-life is not provided as marstacimab
elimination involves both linear and nonlinear clearance mechanisms.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
Body weight was a significant covariate impacting the pharmacokinetics of
marstacimab. Marstacimab exposures over the body weight range 35-120 kg
show a trend for increase in exposure with decrease in body weight. However,
dose adjustment based on body weight is not required.
Age
Based on the population PK analysis, after accounting for the weight difference,
there was minimal difference (3.1%) in CL for adolescents (12 to <18 years old)
and adults.
Renal impairment
No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal
impairment. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody, pharmacokinetics are not
expected to be different in patients with renal impairment.
Hepatic impairment
No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic
impairment. As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody, pharmacokinetics are not
expected to be different in patients with hepatic impairment.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Applicant’s Proposed Dosing Regimen
The proposed dosing regimen of marstacimab is a loading dose of 300 mg by SC injection on
Day 1 followed by maintenance dosage of 150 mg SC injection weekly starting on Day 8. The
maintenance dosage can be adjusted to 300 mg SC injection weekly in patients weighing ≥50 kg
when control of bleeding events is inadequate.
Selection of the Dosing Regimen for the Phase 3 Study
The Applicant’s selection of the proposed dosing regimen for the Phase 3 study was based on the
results of the Phase 1b/2 dose-finding study (Study B7841002). Study B7841002 was conducted
in adult participants with severe hemophilia A or B with and without inhibitors for up to Day 85
(3 months). The study consisted of four marstacimab dose cohorts: (i) 300 mg SC weekly
(Cohort 1; N=7), (ii) 300 mg loading dose, followed by 150 mg SC weekly (Cohort 2; N=6),
(iii) 450 mg SC weekly (Cohort 3; N=6) in participants without inhibitors, and (iv) 300 mg SC
weekly (Cohort 4; N=7) in participants with inhibitors. After multiple doses, the marstacimab
exposure (maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) and AUCtau) increased in greater than doseproportional manner over the range of 150 mg to 450 mg SC weekly. There was no significant
difference in marstacimab pharmacokinetic between participants with or without inhibitors
receiving marstacimab 300 mg weekly doses. The observed ABR up to Day 85 showed

27
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
statistically significant reduction in all dose cohorts compared to the historic factor on-demand3
as well as pretreatment bleeding rates (mean ABR: 4.20, 1.47, 4.17, 0.72 for Cohort 1, 2, 3 and 4,
respectively, while on-study; 27.6 for historic factor on-demand; and 23.0, 14.7, 20.3, 17.3 for
Cohort 1, 2, 3 and 4, respectively, during pretreatment). In general, marstacimab doses of
150 mg to 450 mg weekly were well tolerated. Severe (Grade 3) treatment-related injection site
reactions were reported in two participants for the 450 mg SC weekly regimen. Based on the
results of the Phase 1b/2 study, while both 150 mg and 300 mg weekly doses showed significant
ABR reduction and similar safety profile, a loading dose of 300 mg and maintenance dose of
150 mg weekly regimen, with the option to increase to 300 mg if needed for better bleeding
control, was selected for further evaluation because of greater safety multiples and smaller
injection volume.
Evaluation of the Proposed Dosing Regimen
The efficacy and safety of marstacimab loading dose of 300 mg and maintenance dose of 150 mg
weekly was evaluated in the Phase 3 study (Study B7841005). Dose escalation was also
available to 300 mg maintenance dose if a participant’s bleeding was considered not well
controlled (see subsection below regarding the evaluation of dose adjustment). For participants
who received factor on-demand during the observational phase (N=33), marstacimab prophylaxis
demonstrated superiority over factor on-demand as measured by ABR of treated bleeds (ABR
8.0 and 3.18 for factor on-demand and marstacimab prophylaxis, respectively). For participants
who received factor prophylaxis during the observational phase (N=83), marstacimab
prophylaxis demonstrated noninferiority over factor prophylaxis as measured by ABR of treated
bleeds (ABR 7.85 and 5.08 for factor prophylaxis and marstacimab treatment, respectively).
Refer to Section 6.2 for more details regarding the clinical efficacy of marstacimab. Refer to
Section 7 for details regarding safety of marstacimab. Overall, the proposed dosing regimen
seems reasonable from an efficacy and safety perspective.
Evaluation of Dosing in Adolescents
In Study B7841005, adolescents 12 years of age and older with a minimum body weight of 35 kg
at the time of signing the informed consent form were also enrolled and received the same dosing
regimen as adults. Marstacimab exposures were higher in adolescents compared to adults, likely
due to lower body weight, as body weight is a significant covariate for marstacimab exposure
(refer to Section 14.5, pharmacometrics review). However, the efficacy and safety data from
both adults and adolescents in the study support the proposed dosing regimen. Furthermore,
modeling and simulation using a virtual population of adults and adolescents sampled from the
NHANES database demonstrated that the simulated exposure (median Cmax,ss) in the lowest body
weight group of 30 to 40 kg (65.2 µg/mL) were still below the highest concentration observed in
participants who received marstacimab 450 mg SC weekly (QW) during clinical development
(103 µg/mL in Study B7841002) without any apparent worsening of the safety profile (Figure 5).
The results cover the majority of the body weight distribution for 12-year-old children
3 Per the Applicant, a historical on-demand group was constructed using patients who were on on-demand treatment

in the following Pfizer studies: ReFacto AF 3082B2-4432 (B1831004), BeneFIX B1821010, and BeneFIX 3090A1400 (B1821004). The resulting dataset were further filtered to match the key inclusion/exclusion criteria of Study
B7841002 based on age and factor activity (18≤ age ≤65 and factor activity ≤1%).

(3rd percentile weight is 29.5 kg for 12-year-old male children based on the CDC weight-for-age

study (Study B7841008), which enrolled adolescents ≥12 to <18 years of age with minimum
body weight of 25 kg, no additional safety concern was observed for six participants whose body
weight ranged from 33 to 61.3 kg. Therefore, the proposed dosing regimen for adolescents 12
years and older, which is same as that of the adults, is acceptable regardless of body weight.

Evaluation of the Proposed Dose Adjustment
The Applicant proposed dose increase to marstacimab 300 mg SC weekly in participants
weighing ≥50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the health care
provider. This was implemented in the Phase 3 study (Study B7841005), once the participants
were on stable treatment with marstacimab 150 mg SC weekly for at least 6 months. To evaluate
whether the proposed dose adjustment is reasonable, exposure-response (E-R) analysis was
conducted by FDA for the primary efficacy endpoint of ABR. The E-R analysis was conducted
using LOESS nonparametric regression method with data from Phase 1b/2 study (Study
B7841002), Phase 2 open-label extension study (Study B7841003), and Phase 3 study (Study
B7841005) for doses 150, 300, and 450 mg. Fourteen participants who had dose escalated during
the Phase 3 study were not included due to limited PK samples after dose escalation. However,
the demographic and disease characteristics (i.e., age, antidrug antibody (ADA) status,
hemophilia type, and body weight) of these 14 participants did not show any meaningful

differences to those included in the E-R analysis. The E-R analysis show that there is a slight
trend for decrease in ABR with increase in exposure (Cavg) (Figure 6).

For the body weight cutoff of 50 kg for dose escalation, the simulated exposure for different

simulation, the median Cmax,ss values are 65.2 and 44.6 µg/mL for 30 to <40 kg and 40 to <50 kg,
respectively, at 150 mg weekly dose, and therefore, with a 300 mg dose, the exposure will go
beyond the clinical experience (highest concentration observed with 450 mg weekly
dose =103 µg/mL in Study B7841002) in most participants weighing <50 kg, as the exposure
increases greater than dose-proportionally. For participants weighing 50 kg or more,
marstacimab exposures following 300 mg will stay within the clinical experience. Overall, the
review team agrees with the proposed dose adjustment to marstacimab 300 mg SC weekly in
participants weighing ≥50 kg when control of bleeding events is judged to be inadequate by the
health care provider.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Study B7841005
6.2.1.1. Study Design
Study B7841005 is an open-label, multi-center, two phase (observation and active treatment)
baseline-control study of marstacimab in participants aged 12 years and above with severe HA
and moderately severe to severe HB with or without inhibitors. The primary efficacy objective of
the study is to evaluate the effectiveness of marstacimab prophylaxis compared to factor ondemand t and separately compared to factor prophylaxis in participants with severe HA (FVIII
activity <1%) or moderately severe to severe HB (FIX activity ≤2%) with or without inhibitors.
Study B7841005 enrolled participants with and without inhibitors to separate cohorts. The
subject of this BLA is participants without inhibitors. Participants without inhibitors were
enrolled to two independent cohorts: Cohort OP-OD (observational phase factor on-demand)
enrolled participants with HA and HA without inhibitors who were receiving factor OD for at
least 6 months prior to screening and Cohort OP-PPX (observational phase factor prophylaxis)
enrolled participants who were receiving factor prophylaxis during 6 months prior to enrollment.
The study schema for the noninhibitor cohorts of Study B7841005 is shown in Figure 7. For both
Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX, participants received factor for a 6-month OP. Participants
received on demand (OD) or prophylaxis with FVIII or FIX-replacement therapy for the OP,
depending on the therapy being received prior to enrollment. Upon completion of the OP, all
participants entered a one-year ATP and receive marstacimab prophylaxis. All participants,
regardless of therapy received during the OP, received marstacimab 300 mg SC initial loading
dose followed by 150 mg SC QW. Participants who met protocol-specified dose escalation
criteria based upon breakthrough bleeding, could have their dosage increased to 300 mg SC QW.

31
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Figure 7. Schema for Noninhibitor Cohorts, Study B7841005, Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX

Source: Clinical Reviewer figure
Cohort OP-OD: Participants who received on-demand (OD) treatment with factor replacement therapy during the observational
phase (OP)
Cohort OP-PPX: Participants who received prophylaxis (PPX) treatment with factor replacement therapy during the observational
phase (OP)
Abbreviations: FIX, factor IX; FVIII, factor VIII; HA, hemophilia type A; HB, hemophilia type B

The study design allows a comparison of marstacimab prophylaxis to factor OD and factor
prophylaxis, where each participant serves as his/her control. Efficacy analysis compares ATP
and OP, results for each cohort were independently powered and alpha controlled.
The pooling of participants with HA and HB together within the same cohort is reasonable. In
the coagulation cascade, FVIII and FIX form a single enzymatic complex that goes on to activate
factor X, and thus deficiency of either component (FVIII or FIX) manifests in similar
coagulation deficits. The mechanism of action of marstacimab is independent of which factor is
missing.
The observational period (6 months) of treatment with factor replacement (on-demand or
prophylaxis) is shorter than the active treatment period (12 months) with marstacimab
prophylaxis. Because the bleeding endpoints are based on the annualized bleeding rate with
different durations in OP and ATP, each bleed may carry a different weight when considering the
change of annual bleeding rate (ABR). A set of sensitivity analyses for evaluation of different
durations and also potential seasonal effect is presented for each cohort in Section 6.2.1.5.1.1 and
Section 6.2.1.5.2.1.

32
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria
Key eligibility criteria are summarized in this section, and the full criteria are available in
Section 15.1.
Key Eligibility Criteria
(1) Male age 12 to <75 years inclusive with a minimum body weight of 35 kg.
(2) Diagnosis of severe hemophilia A or moderately severe to severe hemophilia B (FVIII
activity <1% or FIX activity ≤2%, respectively) documented by a clinical laboratory prior to
enrollment.
(3) Subject must also meet the following criteria:
(a) No detectable or documented history of inhibitors against FVIII or FIX prior to
enrollment (baseline of observational phase),
(b) Subjects receiving routine prophylaxis treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement, have
demonstrated at least 80% compliance with scheduled prophylaxis regimen during 6
months prior to enrollment, and willing to continue to receive routine prophylaxis
treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement during the observational phase, or
(c) Subject with on-demand [factor] treatment regimen with ≥6 acute bleeding episodes
(spontaneous and/or traumatic) that required coagulation factor infusion during the 6
months period prior to screening and willing to continue to receive on-demand treatment
during the observational phase. Surgical bleeding episodes do not apply to this criterion.
(4) No known planned surgical procedure during the planned study period.
(5) No abnormal renal or hepatic function
(6) Patient should have platelet count ≥100,000/uL and hemoglobin level ≥10 g/dL.
It is acceptable to enroll only males in this study because hemophilia is seen almost exclusively
in males. There is no basis to expect a different PK, efficacy, or safety profile in female patients,
and therefore, approval should not be limited to male patients.

33
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
6.2.1.3. Study Endpoints
Primary Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint is the annualized bleeding rate (ABR) of treated bleeding events.
•

Cohort OP-OD: The ratio of the mean ABR (marstacimab prophylaxis versus on-demand
treatment) based on adult and adolescent participants meeting the entry criteria with prior ondemand therapy.

•

Cohort OP-PPX: The mean ABR difference (marstacimab prophylaxis – prior prophylactic
treatment) based on adult and adolescent participants meeting the entry criteria with prior
prophylaxis therapy.

In patients with hemophilia, an increased occurrence of bleeding events leads to increased
morbidity and mortality, and thus, a reduction in bleeding events is clinically meaningful.
By focusing on treated bleeds, this endpoint assessed the effect of treatment on the most serious
and clinically meaningful bleeding events.
Key Secondary Endpoints
•

Incidence of spontaneous bleeds – spontaneous bleed is defined as bleeding for no
apparent/known reason particularly into the joints, muscles, and soft tissues as opposed to
traumatic bleed (i.e., bleeding event occurring for an apparent/known reason). Only treated
spontaneous bleeds were counted.
Reduction in spontaneous bleeds, as opposed to bleeds that occur after trauma, is clinically
meaningful to patients and prevention or reduction of spontaneous bleeds is one of the goals
of modern hemophilia management.

•

Incidence of joint bleeds – joint bleed is defined as a bleeding episode characterized by rapid
loss of range of motion as compared with baseline that is associated with any combination of
the following: pain or an unusual sensation in the joint, palpable swelling, and warmth of the
skin over the joint. Only treated joint bleeds were counted.
Repeat bleeding into a joint can be debilitating and over time result in hemophilic
arthropathy. Reducing and preventing joint bleeds is beneficial to patients to help prevent the
late complication of hemophilic arthropathy.

•

Incidence of total bleeds (treated and untreated) – total bleeds include both treated and
untreated bleeds. An untreated bleed is considered a “new untreated bleed” if
occurring >48 hours after the previous new untreated bleed at the same site and
occurring >72 hours after stopping treatment from the original bleed for which treatment was
initiated; an untreated bleed occurring at a different site is considered a “new untreated
bleed” regardless of the time of the bleed.
Reduction of both treated and untreated bleeds is clinically meaningful to patients as any
bleed impacts patients with hemophilia.

34
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
•

Incidence of target joint bleeds – target joint bleed is defined as a major joint (e.g., hip,
elbow, wrist, shoulder, knee, and ankle) into which repeated bleeds occur (three or more
spontaneous bleeds into a single joint within a consecutive 6-month period). To derive the
“incidence” of target joint bleeds in each of the OP and ATP, “baseline” target joints were
predetermined before the start of the OP. Only treated target joint bleeds were counted.
Once joint damage and inflammation occurs due to hemarthrosis, a joint can develop
increased susceptibility to further bleeding, becoming a target joint. Reducing bleeds in target
joints is a clinical benefit to prevent further damage and development of hemophilic
arthropathy.

•

Physical health domain in the Haemo-Qol (< 17 years of age) and the Hematology A Quality
of Life (Haem-A-QoL) (≥17 years of age) at 6 months. The Haem-A-Qol and Haemo-Qol
PROs are designed to assess health-related quality of life in patients with hemophilia. There
were not enough participants < 17 years of age to analyze the PRO in adolescents. The adult
version, Haem-A-QoL, consists of 46 items comprising 10 domains. There are 5 items in the
physical health domain assessing painful swelling, joint pain, painful movement, difficulty
walking, and needing more time to get ready. Each item is scored on a 5-point Likert scale (1
= “never”, 2 = “rarely”, 3 = “sometimes”, 4 = “often”, and 5 = “all the time”) and utilizes a
recall period of the “past 4 weeks.” For reporting purposes, the individual items are
combined to scales (e.g., physical health) and the scales were combined to form an overall
total score ranging from 0 to 100, with lower scores indicating better physical health status.

•

The Haem-A-QOL Physical Health tool has been used in a prior approval in the treatment of
hemophilia and included in the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) for that
product, although with a different set of items.
6.2.1.4. Statistical Analysis Plan

The statistical analysis plan (SAP) for the study was finalized on January 31, 2023, and the data
cutoff date was April 17, 2023. Key information regarding statistical analysis from the clinical
study report, protocol, and SAP is summarized below.
Analysis Population
The following analyses sets were defined for the statistical analysis.
(1) Entered analysis set: All participants who sign the informed consent document.
(2) Enrolled analysis set: All entered participants assigned to a treatment in OP.
(3) All safety analysis set: For participants with prior prophylaxis in OP, all participants who
received at least 1 prophylaxis or on-demand treatment at OP; for participants with ondemand in OP, all participants who completed any of the procedures for Visit 2 (OP
baseline). Participants who changed from a noninhibitor to an inhibitor on or before ATP
Day -7 testing were excluded from this population.

35
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
(4) Marstacimab safety analysis set: All participants who were administered at least one dose of
marstacimab during the ATP.
(5) Modified intent-to-treat (mITT) analysis set: All participants who completed OP and
received at least one dose of marstacimab in ATP (excluding participants with inhibitors who
were treated with factor prophylaxis in the OP). Participants who changed from a
noninhibitor to inhibitor on or before ATP Day -7 testing were excluded from mITT. This
population was utilized for all efficacy analyses.
In this review, the population used for the primary analysis (defined as mITT above) is referred
to as the efficacy analysis set, and included participants who had both OP and ATP data. Because
the primary analyses were based on the comparison between OP and ATP, the efficacy analysis
set could not be based on all participants with OP data. With only less than 10% of participants
who did not have ATP data, the impact of efficacy analysis was minimum.
Sample Size Determination
For adequate powering, the Applicant planned a sample size of 25 participants when
marstacimab prophylaxis was compared with on-demand treatment to demonstrate superiority
and 60 participants when marstacimab prophylaxis was compared with factor prophylaxis for
noninferiority.
These sample sizes were derived via simulation under a range of scenarios. In the simulation,
bleeding counts were generated from a negative binomial distribution with the mean bleeds over
6 months of 12.5 for factor on-demand. The mean bleeds over 6 months for factor prophylaxis
was initially assumed as 2.5 with an added scenario of 2.35 that was obtained as the weighted
average of hemophilia A and B ABRs based on the meta-analysis; the weights were based on the
prevalence of each hemophilia type. The mean bleeds over 12 months for marstacimab
prophylaxis was initially assumed as 4 with two additional scenarios of 2.67 and 3.46, the point
estimate and the upper bound of the 1-sided 80% CI from Study B7841002, respectively. The
variance was assumed as 6 times the mean for each scenario; the correlation between the two
bleeding counts at OP and ATP from the same participant was assumed as 0.2.
The simulation further accounted for different follow-up times due to drop-out or lost-to-followup. To simulate 10% discontinuation during the ATP, a uniform (0, 1) random number Ui was
generated for each participant; when Ui <0.1, the duration of treatment was curtailed into 10 • 𝑈𝑖
years; the number of bleeding Xi in this reduced duration was newly generated from a negative
binomial distribution with the expected value of 𝜇𝑖 =10 • 𝑈𝑖 • 𝜇 and variance of 6 • 𝜇𝑖 where 𝜇
represented the mean bleeding for marstacimab prophylaxis for complete follow up. Table 11
below demonstrated that the planned sample sizes maintain adequate power (>90%) for all
scenarios except for the most pessimistic case.

versus

Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint was the ABR of treated bleeding events, which was derived for
each participant using the following formula: ABR = number of bleeds requiring
treatments/(days on treatment period/365.25). If a participant did not complete a treatment
period, the days on treatment ended at the last dosing date+6 days.
Superiority Versus Factor-Based On-Demand Treatment
A repeated measure negative binomial regression model via generalized estimating equation
approach was used with log link function. In the model, the number of bleeds was a response
variable, and treatment (marstacimab prophylaxis or factor-based on-demand) was a factor. The
time on treatment in log years was an offset variable to account for different duration on
treatment. The working correlation was set as unstructured. The superiority criterion of
marstacimab prophylaxis over factor-based on-demand is if the 2-sided 95% confidence interval
(CI) for the mean ABR ratio (marstacimab prophylaxis/factor-based on-demand) was below the
preset threshold of 0.5.
The Applicant proposed the superiority criterion for comparison versus on-demand factor
treatment for the primary and key secondary endpoints related to bleeding to be 0.5 in the SAP.
The review team considered a threshold of 0.5 acceptable because it is more conservative than a
commonly accepted criterion of 1.
Noninferiority Versus Factor Prophylaxis
A repeated measure negative binomial regression model via generalized estimating equation
approach was used with identity link function. In the model, the number of bleeds was a response
variable, and duration (in years) and the interaction by treatment (marstacimab prophylaxis or
37
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
factor prophylaxis) and duration were factors. The working correlation was set as unstructured.
Treatment difference in the mean ABRs was obtained using a contrast within the interaction
term. The noninferiority criterion of marstacimab prophylaxis over factor prophylaxis was if the
upper bound of the 2-sided 95% CI for the mean ABR difference (marstacimab prophylaxis –
factor prophylaxis) lies below the preset noninferiority (NI) margin 2.5 for primary endpoint.
The review team considered this NI margin acceptable. Refer to Section 16.1 regarding the
Applicant’s derivation of the NI margin 2.5 for the primary endpoint.
Subgroup Analysis for the Primary Endpoint
Results for the primary endpoint ABR of treated bleeds, when compared with on-demand and
factor prophylaxis treatment for participants without inhibitors, were summarized by hemophilia
types, age, race, ethnicity, and geographic region, respectively.
Sensitivity/Supplementary Analysis for the Primary Endpoint
Sensitivity/supplementary analyses of the ABR of treated bleeds were conducted to support the
conclusion of the primary analysis with robustness under different handlings of intercurrent
events (preventative infusion, dose increase, treatment discontinuation, or potentially missing
records) and potential concerns for bias (due to carryover effect or seasonal effect) from the
primary analysis.
Impact of Seasonal and Carryover Effects
There was no washout period in the one-way cross-over design of Study B7841005 and the
comparison between the 6-month OP and 12-month ATP resulted in potential seasonal effect on
the primary endpoint.
The Applicant conducted the following sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint (ABR for
treated bleeds).
(1) The main analysis was repeated with the same model for the primary analysis by comparing
6-month OP versus excluding the first month of marstacimab treatment during the ATP.
(2) The main analysis was repeated with the same model for the primary analysis by comparing
6-month OP versus data only matched the calendar days in ATP.
(3) The bleed rates were compared descriptively during the early part of the treatment period
(Months 1 to 6 in ATP) and the latter part of the treatment period (Months 7 to 12 ATP).
The review team conducted additional sensitivity analyses by comparing the 6-month OP with
the second half ATP to evaluate the robustness of the primary analysis results.

38
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Handling of Intercurrent Events for the Primary Endpoint
The Applicant performed additional supplementary analyses using the same model for the
primary endpoint with following approaches to dealing with intercurrent events and
discontinuation.
(1) To assess the impact of preventative treatment for medical/dental procedures, sport activity
or physical therapy (plus 72 hours): include bleeding during preventative infusion.
(2) To assess the impact of dose increase, the main analysis was repeated with the same model,
using the following approaches:
(a) Including data after dose increase.
(b) Imputing the portion after dose increase using the mean ABR calculated before the dose
increase to give full weight in the negative binomial regression to the observations from
participants with such treatment.
(c) Tipping point analysis using ABRs calculated combining bleeds during the intervention
dose and assuming the bleed rate after the dose increases as multiples of successively
larger (>1) factors of the former to determine the tipping point.
(3) To assess the impact of treatment discontinuation, the main analysis was repeated with the
same model, using the following approaches:
(a) Imputing the portion after treatment discontinuation using the mean ABR calculated
based on bleeds during treatment to give full weight in the negative binomial regression
to the observations from those participants.
(b) Tipping point analysis using ABRs calculated combining bleeds during the treatment and
assuming the bleed rate after the treatment discontinuation as multiples of successively
larger (>1) factors of the former to determine the tipping point.
Although the Applicant considered dose increase as an intercurrent event, because dose increase
was allowed per protocol, the Division did not agree, and sensitivity and tipping point analyses
for dose increase are thus not included with the review. An evaluation of the proposed dose
adjustment (i.e., increase) is included in Section 6.1.
Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints
The first four key secondary endpoints of incidence of (1) spontaneous bleeds, (2) joint bleeds,
(3) total bleeds either treated or untreated, and (4) target join bleeds were similarly derived as the
derivation of the ABR of treated bleeds and were analyzed using the same negative binomial
model as that for the primary endpoint.
The estimand for the bleeding-related key secondary endpoints had the same attributes of
treatment, population, intercurrent events, and population-level summary as in the primary
estimand. The Applicant did not conduct sensitivity or supplemental analysis for the bleedingrelated key secondary endpoints.
Another key secondary endpoint was change from baseline in physical health domain in the
Haem-A-QoL at 6 months. For reporting purposes, the individual 5 items in the Haem-A-QoL
39
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
were combined to domain scales (e.g., physical health) and the domain scales were combined to
form an overall total score. The transformed scale score (TSS) on a scale of 0 to 100 was
calculated using the following formula and used for the analyses.
Equation 1. Transformed Scale Score Calculation

TSS = 100 ×

raw score ― minimal possible raw score
maximum possible raw score ― minimum possible raw score

Source: Study B7841005 SAP v6 dated January 31, 2023
Abbreviations: SAP, statistical analysis plan; TSS, transformed scale score

Each TSS reflects the average response of the valid items when there were at least a minimum
required number of valid items. Otherwise, the TSS is set as missing. To compute the TSS for
the physical health domain, a minimum of three out of five valid items is required.
The Wilcoxon signed rank test was used to perform the superiority and/or noninferiority
comparison. Missing observations were handled via multiple imputation approach under missing
at random assumption. Sensitivity tipping point analyses would also be conducted via
successively applying pessimistic assumptions for missingness due to withdrawals because of
adverse event (AE) or lack of efficacy.
Hierarchical Testing Sequence and Multiple Testing Strategy
The ordering of statistical testing for Study B7841005, Cohort OP-OD and Cohort OP-PPX
proposed in the SAP is shown in Table 12 (column SAP). During the course of the review, the
Applicant clarified that they were seeking to establish substantial evidence of effectiveness based
on two independent and completed cohorts conducted under a single protocol, B7841005. The
Agency agreed with the characterization of the two cohorts as the equivalent of independent
substudies, allowing them to be analyzed separately. As a result, individual alphas with 0.05
could be given to each cohort separately. The new hierarchical sequences based on independent
analysis of the OP-OD and OP-PPX cohorts, which is the sequence used for this review, is
shown in Table 12 (column review).
As seen in the table, the testing of superiority over factor prophylaxis in the OP-PPX cohort was
not dependent on the results of noninferiority testing of physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL
in the OP-PPX. If noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis was established for all bleeding
endpoints, testing for superiority versus factor prophylaxis may be conducted using the same
statistical model in a way to control the Type I error rate.

6.2.1.5.1.1. Superiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over OnDemand Factor (OP)
Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint
In Study B7841005, the primary efficacy endpoint was ABR of treated bleeds. The model based
ABR was 3.18 for marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP and 38.00 for on-demand factor
during the OP. The ABR ratio is 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119) with the p-value<0.0001, showing
a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio was less
than 0.5, thus meeting the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over on-demand
factor. These results are summarized in Table 17.

factor was consistent by hemophilia type and age groups. Of note, HB subgroup had a wide

At the request of the Agency, the Applicant conducted additional analyses based on further

factor prophylaxis during the OP. The results showed a consistent pattern across different
subgroups (i.e., age, race, ethnicity, and geographic region). Refer to Section 16.2 for more
information.
Subgroup analysis was not conducted for different countries as the 33 participants in the OP-OD
cohort were enrolled at over 9 countries, making subgroups too small to yield meaningful
analysis. Note that there were no U.S. participants in the on-demand factor cohort.
Sensitivity/Supplementary Analyses Results of the Primary Endpoint
To ensure the robustness of the primary analysis results, the following sensitivity/supplementary
analyses were conducted.

45
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Sensitivity Analysis Results for Evaluation of Carry-Over and Seasonal Effects
The sensitivity analysis results for evaluation of carry-over and seasonal effects for Cohort OPOD are shown in Table 18.
The study did not include a washout period between the OP with factor and initiation of the ATP
with marstacimab. To account for possible carry-over effect, the Applicant compared the ABR
rate for the 6-month OP and the ABR rate for the ATP, excluding the first month of treatment
with marstacimab. As seen in Table 18, the ABR ratio of marstacimab versus OD using the full
ATP period was 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119) compared to 0.082 (95% CI: 0.057, 0.118) when
the first month of ATP is excluded. These similar estimated ABR ratios versus OD demonstrated
there was no carry-over effect from the treatment with factor during the OP to treatment with
marstacimab during ATP.
To compare the potential impact of seasonality, the Applicant compared the 6-month OP ABR
with an ATP ABR based on the matched calendar days and with an ATP ABR based on the
second 6-month period of the ATP. While similar, the matched calendar days and second half
ATP are not the same for participants whose treatment duration are not exact 180 days during OP
and 365 days during ATP. For both comparisons, as seen in the table, the ATP ABR for a subset
of the ATP period is lower than the ATP ABR for the entire ATP period. This results in smaller
ratios versus OD. For the calendar matched ATP, the ratio OD is 0.062 (95% CI: 0.043, 0.090)
and for the second half ATP, the ratio versus OD is 0.063 (95% CI: 0.041, 0.098) compared to the
ratio OD based on the entire ATP of 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119). This suggests there may be
some seasonality; however, the CIs are overlapping and thus no conclusion can be drawn. Most
importantly, the upper bound of the 95% CIs for the ABR ratios for both the full ATP and the
calendar matched ATP were less than 0.5, the predetermined superiority criterion for superiority,
and thus the sensitivity analysis results supported that the superiority criterion versus on-demand
factor for the primary endpoint was met.
The Applicant also conducted analyses for the primary endpoint based on the first and second
halves of ATP (Table 19) for completed participants who remained on a dose of 150 mg
marstacimab SC QW. The mean ATP ABR based on the first 6 months of the ATP (3.99
[SD =2]) is larger than the ATP ABR based on the second 6 months of the ATP (2.42
[SD =3.024]); however, the medians are nearly identical (2.0 for the first 6 months compared to
2.01 for the second 6 months). This suggests the higher ABR during the first 6 months is due to
an extreme value, which is confirmed where the max ABR for the first 6 months was 35.93
compared to 10.15 during the second 6 months.
Overall, the Division concludes that neither lack of washout period nor seasonality changed the
conclusion that superiority versus on-demand factor is met for marstacimab prophylaxis.

Supplementary Analysis Results for Handling of Intercurrent Events
Intercurrent events included preventative infusion and treatment discontinuation or potentially
missing bleeding records.
•

Preventative infusion. By including bleeding during preventative infusion, the model based
ABR ratio was 0.083 (95% CI: 0.058, 0.118), similar to results of the primary analysis.

•

Treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records. There was one participant
who had a dosing gap (≥35 days), during which time no bleed was reported. When data
during dose gap was imputed using the mean ABR, the model based ABR ratio was 0.082
(95% CI: 0.058, 0.117), similar to results of the primary analysis.

•

The tipping point analysis assumed the bleed rate during the dose gap (≥35 days) as multiples
of successively larger (>1) factors of the ABRs during the observed portions. The results

demonstrated that unless the tipping multiplier was 154 or higher, the conclusion regarding

efficacy analysis results for data of marstacimab prophylaxis superiority comparison with factor
on-demand. Refer to Section 16.3 for a summary table of the supplementary analyses results.

Spontaneous Bleeds
The model based ABR was 2.44 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 30.93 for on-demand
factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.079 (95% CI: 0.054, 0.114) with the p-value<0.0001,
showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio
was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over ondemand factor.
Joint Bleeds
The model based ABR was 2.83 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 32.86 for on-demand
factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.086 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.125) with the p-value<0.0001,
showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio
was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over ondemand factor.
Total Bleeds
The model based ABR was 7.39 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 47.76 for on-demand
factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.155 (95% CI: 0.116, 0.207) with the p-value<0.0001,
showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio
was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over ondemand factor.
Target Joint Bleeds
The model based ABR was 1.84 for marstacimab during the ATP, and 23.18 for on-demand
factor during the OP. The ABR ratio was 0.079 (95% CI: 0.051, 0.124) with the p-value<0.0001,
showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR ratio
was less than 0.5 and achieved the predetermined superiority criterion for marstacimab over ondemand factor.
Physical Health Domain in Haem-A-QoL at 6 Months
The model based median score change from baseline in physical health domain in the Haem-AQoL at 6 months was -12.35 for marstacimab during the ATP, and -1.12 for factor on-demand
during the OP. The estimated difference was -10.678 (95% CI: -24.001, 2.645) with the pvalue=0.1161. A negative difference indicates an improvement in quality of life related to
physical health. However, the estimated difference of -10.678 was associated with 95% CI that
crossed 0, with a p-value=0.1161, and thus is not considered statistically significant.
For the Haemo-QoL (adolescents 12 to 17 years) physical health domain score, there were not
enough participants (N=2) for any formal statistical analysis. The analyses of change from
baseline in physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL (adults ≥17 years) at 6 months was based on
31 out of 33 participants.

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
No participant discontinued the study due to lack of efficacy or adverse event; thus, the tipping
point analysis of change from baseline at 6 months in physical health domain of the Haem-AQoL for on-demand treatment at OP was not performed.
At the request of the Agency, the Applicant conducted nonparametric analyses for the primary
and key secondary endpoints related to ABR to assess the robustness of the primary analysis
results. Their results based on the nonparametric approach (i.e., Wilcoxon signed rank test) for
the ABR differences (ATP - OP) showed p-value<0.0001 for all major efficacy endpoints related
to ABR. The review team confirmed these results and therefore concluded that the primary
analysis results for on-demand superiority comparisons are positive with statistical significance.
6.2.1.5.2. Prophylaxis Factor Therapy During Observation

Patient Disposition
A total of 91 participants with prior treatment with factor prophylaxis entered the study. Of
those, a total of 84 (92.3%) participants completed the 6-month OP. Seven participants
discontinued during the OP, 2 of them due to protocol deviation and 5 due to no longer meeting
the eligibility criteria. Among the 84 participants who completed the OP, 83 entered the 12month ATP treatment with marstacimab. These 83 participants composed the efficacy analysis
set for Cohort OP-PPX. During the ATP, five participants discontinued marstacimab, 4 of them
withdrew and 1 discontinued due to an AE.

6.2.1.5.2.1. Noninferiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over
Prophylaxis With Factor (OP)
Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint
In Study B7841005, the primary efficacy endpoint was ABR of treated bleeds. The model based
ABR was 5.08 for marstacimab during the ATP and 7.85 for prophylaxis with factor during the
OP. The ABR difference was -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16). The upper bound of the 95% CI for
the ABR difference was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin of 2.5, thus meeting the
predetermined noninferiority criterion of marstacimab over prophylaxis with factor. These
results are summarized in Table 24. The review team also evaluated the assay sensitivity (i.e., the
factor prophylaxis during the OP’s performance) to ensure that NI results are interpretable.
Detailed examination is shown in Section 16.4.

and factor prophylaxis at the OP, to bracket around. In general, the results showed consistent

subgroups: age (ages 12 to <18, N=17) had model based ABR difference -0.34 and 95% CI (3.23, 2.55) and North America (N=11) had model based ABR difference 0.43 and 95% CI (2.43, 3.29), where the upper bound of the 95% CI exceeded the noninferiority margin 2.5. This
