cohort were enrolled at 14 countries, making subgroups too small to yield meaningful analysis.
Note that there was only one participant enrolled at a U.S. site.
Sensitivity/Supplementary Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
To ensure the robustness of the primary analysis results, the Applicant conducted the following
sensitivity/supplementary analyses.
Sensitivity Analysis for Evaluation of Carry-Over and Seasonal Effects
The sensitivity analysis results for evaluation of carry-over and seasonal effects for Cohort OPOD are shown in Table 25. The study did not include a washout period between the OP with
factor prophylaxis and initiation of the ATP with marstacimab prophylaxis. To account for
possible carry-over effect, the Applicant compared the ABR rate for the 6-month OP and the
ABR rate for the ATP, excluding the first month of treatment with marstacimab. As seen in
Table 25, the difference versus factor prophylaxis using the full ATP period is -2.77 (95% CI: 5.37, -0.16) compared to -2.56 (95% CI: -5.26, 0.13) when the first month of ATP is excluded.
The similarity in difference versus factor prophylaxis with and without the first month of ATP
demonstrated there was no carry-over effect from the treatment with factor prophylaxis during
the OP to marstacimab prophylaxis during ATP.
To compare the potential impact of seasonality, the Applicant compared the 6-month OP ABR
with an ATP ABR based on the matched calendar days and with an ATP ABR based on the
second 6-month period of the ATP. While similar, the matched calendar days and second half
ATP are not the same for participants whose treatment duration are not exactly 180 days during
OP and 365 days during ATP. For both comparisons, as seen in the table, the ATP ABR for a
subset of the full ATP is lower than the ATP ABR for the entire ATP period. This results in
smaller differences versus factor prophylaxis. For the matched calendar days ATP, the difference
versus. factor prophylaxis is -4.40 (95% CI: -7.10, -1.70) and for the second half ATP, the
difference versus factor prophylaxis is -4.92 (95% CI: -7.69, -2.14) compared to -2.77 (95% CI: 5.37, -0.16) for the full ATP.
This suggests there may be some seasonality; however, the CIs are overlapping and thus no
conclusion can be drawn. Importantly, the upper bound of the 95% CIs for the ABR difference
for both comparisons were less than the noninferiority margin 2.5. Thus, the sensitivity analysis
results supported that primary analysis that the noninferiority criterion versus factor prophylaxis
cohort for the primary endpoint was met.
54
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
The Applicant also conducted analyses for the primary endpoint (ABR of treated bleeds) of the
first and second half of ATP (Table 26) for completed participants who remained on a dose of
150 mg marstacimab SC QW, comparing factor prophylaxis during the OP and marstacimab
prophylaxis during the ATP. Although the mean ABR of participants was lower in the second
half of ATP compared with that in the first half of ATP (2.82 versus 3.54, respectively), the
median ABR during the first and second half of ATP were similar. This supports that there is no
concern in terms of seasonal effect.
Overall, the Division concludes that neither lack of washout period nor seasonality changed the
conclusion that noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis is met for marstacimab prophylaxis.

Supplementary Analysis for Handling of Intercurrent Events
Intercurrent events included preventative infusion and treatment discontinuation or potentially
missing bleeding records.
•

Preventative infusion: By including bleeding during preventative infusion, the model based
ABR difference was -2.82 (95% CI: -5.43, -0.20) and compatible with results of the primary
analysis.

•

Treatment discontinuation or potentially missing bleeding records: There were six
participants with ATP discontinuation or dosing gap (≥35 days) during which time no bleed
was reported. When data after treatment discontinuation was imputed using the mean ABR
from prior to discontinuation and data during a dosing gap was imputed using the mean ABR
from before and after the dosing gap, the model based ABR difference was -3.12 (95% CI: 5.73, -0.51). The tipping point analysis assumed the bleed rate after study discontinuation or
during the dose gap (≥35 days) as multiples of successively larger (>1) factors of the ABRs
during the observed portions. The results demonstrated that unless the tipping multiplier was
4.6 or higher, the conclusion regarding noninferiority over factor prophylaxis is the same. A
tipping multiplier of 4.6 is considered high, and therefore, we conclude the results are robust.

Refer to Section 16.3 for a summary table of the supplementary analyses results.

Since the upper bound of the 95% CI for the ABR difference for the primary endpoint was less
than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5 and met the noninferiority criterion of
marstacimab prophylaxis compared with factor prophylaxis, the following key secondary
efficacy endpoints for comparison versus factor prophylaxis were tested in the prespecified
hierarchical order:
•

•

•

Thus, the noninferiority criteria were met for all key secondary endpoints related to ABR.

The change from baseline at 6 months in physical health domain in Haem-A-QoL could not be

endpoint (treated bleeds) versus factor prophylaxis was not robust using different parametric and
nonparametric methods and thus could not be established (See Section 16.4 ). The estimated
median difference was -2.2 (95% CI: -9.1, 4.6).
6.2.1.5.2.2. Superiority of Marstacimab (ATP) Over
Prophylaxis With Factor (OP)
Although the primary endpoint for superiority over prophylaxis with factor for the OP-PPX
cohort could be tested based on the hierarchical testing sequence that considered the two cohorts
independently, the results were not found to be robust. Demonstration of superiority was not
necessary for the Agency’s ultimate regulatory decision, and the Applicant did not seek a claim
of superiority over factor prophylaxis in the proposed USPI.
For completeness, detailed analysis of superiority over factor prophylaxis are presented in
Section 16.4.
6.2.1.5.3. Additional Assessments, Cohorts OP-OD and
OP-PPX
Impact of Financial Compensation at One Site
During the BLA review, the review team noted that participants from OMAN site 1091 received
financial compensation. Among the five participants in site 1091, there was one participant
enrolled to the OP-OD cohort and four participants enrolled to the OP-PPX. For both superiority
versus the OP-OD cohort and noninferiority versus OP-PPX, the review team conducted
additional sensitivity analyses by different imputation methods (replacing the ABR value by 0,
mean, and median of the remaining participants’ ABRs), the results remained statistically
significant and supported the primary analysis results.
Applicability of Results to the U.S. Population
Only one participant from the United States was enrolled in Study B7841005 Cohort OP-PPX
and no participants from the United States in Cohort OP-OD. Overall, there were 15 participants
enrolled in the North America region (6 participants from Canada, 8 participants from Mexico,
and one participant from the United States). The Applicant attributed low enrollment in the
United States mainly to early shutdown during the COVID-19 pandemic and later resumption of
enrollment in the United States compared to other regions. The Applicant asserts that the results
from Study B7841005 are applicable to the U.S. population given the well-known etiology of
HA and HB, similarity of treatment paradigm of hemophilia globally, and similarity between the
population enrolled to Study B7841005 population and to the U.S. hemophilia population with
respect to demographic, disease severity, available therapies, and response to treatment. A
58
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
subgroup analysis based on geographic regions of the efficacy of marstacimab prophylaxis found
the ABRs were numerically lower in North America (2.57) and the Middle East (2.98), followed
by Europe (4.52), and numerically higher in Asia (7.55).
The clinical team concurs with the Applicant’s justification that hemophilia is primarily a
congenital disease caused by deficiency in coagulation factor VIII or IX. The current standard
treatment based on factor replacement (on-demand or prophylaxis) is consistent across global
regions. The World Federation of Hemophilia guidelines for the management of hemophilia do
not adjust the guidelines based on geographical location. The high-level subgroup analysis of
ABR based on region does not demonstrate a higher ABR in North America which would
suggest possible lower efficacy. Therefore, the review team concludes that the results of Study
B7841005 are applicable to the U.S. population.
6.2.1.6. Conclusions of Benefit
In Cohort OP-OD analysis of superiority of marstacimab over factor on-demand, the model
based ABR ratio for the primary endpoint (treated bleeds) was 0.084 (95% CI: 0.059, 0.119)
with the p-value<0.0001, showing a statistically significant test result. The upper bound of the
95% CI for the ABR ratio was less than 0.5, the preselected superiority criterion. The model
based ABR ratios for the first four key secondary endpoints spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds,
total bleeds, and target joint bleeds were 0.079 (95% CI: 0.054, 0.114), 0.086 (95% CI: 0.059,
0.125), 0.155 (95% CI: 0.116, 0.207), 0.079 (95% CI: 0.051, 0.124), with the p-value<0.0001,
respectively. Thus, the superiority criteria of marstacimab over factor on-demand for major
efficacy endpoints related to ABR were achieved.
In Cohort OP-PPX analysis for noninferiority of marstacimab over factor prophylaxis, the model
based ABR difference for the treated bleeds was -2.77 (95% CI: -5.37, -0.16) with upper bound
of the 95% CI less than the prespecified noninferiority margin 2.5. The model based ABR
differences for the key secondary endpoints spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds, total bleeds, and
target joint bleeds were 2.087 (-95% CI: 4.232, 0.060), -1.527 (95% CI: -3.698, 0.644), -2.868
(95% CI: -5.613, -0.122), -0.856 (95% CI: -2.413, 0.701), respectively. The upper bounds of the
CIs for the difference were less than the prespecified noninferiority margins, which were 2.5 for
spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds; 1.2 for target joint bleeds; 2.9 for total bleeds. Thus, the
noninferiority criteria of marstacimab over factor prophylaxis for all major efficacy endpoints
related to ABR were achieved. The sensitivity and supplemental analyses demonstrated that the
final results for the primary efficacy endpoints were robust.
Patients with HA without factors and HB without factors have limited treatment options for the
prevention (prophylaxis) and treatment (on-demand or episodic) of bleeds. Greater number of
bleeding events leads to increased morbidity and mortality. Marstacimab is a first-in-class human
monoclonal antibody that neutralizes TFPI activity and enhances coagulation.
Prophylactic treatment that prevents or reduces bleeding, especially joint bleeds and intracerebral
hemorrhage, is important for long-term outcomes, in particular prevention of joint arthropathy, in
patients with hemophilia. Factor prophylaxis has risks which include the need for recurrent
venous access, and the frequency of infusions can be burdensome to patients. Additionally, there
is a risk for the development of inhibitors with factor replacement. Marstacimab provides for a
59
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
nonfactor prophylaxis option with a subcutaneous weekly dosing regimen for patients with
hemophilia A and B without inhibitors, decreasing treatment burden and eliminating the risk of
developing inhibitors to factor. Additionally, marstacimab prophylaxis is superior to on-demand
factor therapy for patients who are not on a prophylaxis regimen.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. The ATP ABR Point Estimates and 95% CI in
Hemophilia B Compared to Hemophilia A in Cohort
OP-PPX
Issue
Observed smaller ABR difference between ATP ABR and OD ABR in participants with HB
compared to HB for Cohort OP-PPX
Background
As seen in Figure 9, in the OP-PPX cohort, the mean ABR difference in participants with HA
(N=65) is -3.91 (95% CI: -7.10, -7.73) and in participants with HB (N=18) is 1.35 (95% CI:
-1.44, 4.13). Thus, the mean ABR difference and upper bound of the 95% CI in participants with
HA are below the noninferiority margin. However, in participants with HB, while the mean ABR
difference is below the noninferiority margin, the upper bound of the 95% CI is above the
margin.
Assessment
In response to the Agency’s requests for additional information regarding the observed lower
point estimate for the ABR difference for the HB subgroup compared to the HA subgroup, the
Applicant provided the following possible explanation:
•

The observation could be due to four participants in the HB subgroup with unexpectedly high
ABRs during the ATP (ABR >10). These four participants comprise two adolescents and two
adults. Both adolescents and one adult dose escalated to 300 mg SC QW during the ATP. All
four participants continued into the long-term extension Study B7841007. In looking at this
further, the higher mean ABR for participants with HB during the ATP was driven primarily

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
between Study Days 54 and 314. Six of the bleeds involved joints and five involved soft tissue or

become more active during the ATP.
Conclusion
The review team concludes that despite some observed differences in point estimate of the ABR
difference in participants with HA compared to HB, marstacimab prophylaxis demonstrates
efficacy in both populations. The information provided by the Applicant for specific participants
with HB who had extreme ABRs during the ATP provided plausible reasons why these
individuals may have had higher bleeds during ATP when receiving marstacimab compared to
during OP when receiving factor prophylaxis. Two participants with poor bleed control on
150 mg marstacimab prophylaxis during the ATP compared to factor prophylaxis during the OP
appeared to have markedly improved ABR when the marstacimab dose was increased to 300 mg.
One additional participant with HB who had a much higher ABR during ATP than OP was noted
as being more active during ATP, potentially contributing to the higher point estimate observed
in participants with HB.
Given the overall small sample size of the OP-PPX cohort, subgroups by hemophilia type were
small and the study was not powered to draw statistical conclusions on subgroups. For both HA
and HB, the point estimate of the ABR difference did not exceed the prespecified noninferiority
margin. While the 95% CI for the mean ABR difference for the subgroup of participants with
HB was above the noninferiority margin, we note that this margin was selected for use with the
entire OP-PPX cohort (HA and HB). Reassurance of the efficacy of marstacimab in participants
with HB is further provided by the observation in the OP-OD cohort where the ATP ABR was
significantly reduced compared to the OP ABR (ABR =23.49 (95% CI: 19.28, 28,63) versus
ATP ABR of 0.86 (95% CI: 0.26, 2.8)). Based on mechanism of action of marstacimab, which is
independent of which factor is deficiency (FVIII for HA or FIX for HB), one would not expect a
difference in effect between HA and HB. Overall, the review team concludes there is a treatment
effect of marstacimab prophylaxis in participants with HA and in participants with HB.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Marstacimab binds to human TFPI, specifically at an epitope on the Kunitz-type serine protease
inhibitor K2 domain (K2), with affinity in the low- to sub-nanomolar range (Section 13.1) but
does not bind the K1 or K3 domains of TFPI. Marstacimab showed similar binding affinity to the
combined K1K2 domains (via the K2 domain) of TFPI from human, cynomolgus monkey,
rabbit, mouse, and the Wistar Han rat.
Toxicology studies of 3 months duration that utilized high doses of marstacimab in healthy rats
(up to 1,000 mg/kg/week, representing >200-times the clinical exposure at the maximum
62
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
recommended weekly human dose (MRHD) and in healthy monkeys (up to 500 mg/kg/week,
representing >200-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on the AUC) caused an
increase in D-dimer and increased the incidence of microscopic emboli in the lung and injection
site, but no significantly adverse thrombi were identified by histopathology. A low incidence of
acute minimal lung emboli was observed sporadically at the end of the 6-month rat study at 60
(1F, 1M), 180 (4F, 2M) and 1000 mg/kg (1F, 0M) (versus 0F and 0M with the vehicle control),
and across other studies (Table 75). These emboli were of uncertain clinical significance because
they (a) lacked a clear dose-response relationship, (b) were observed in the absence of adverse
secondary lesions in the lungs (i.e., there was no evidence of any lesions significant enough to
damage lung tissues or impact lung function), (c) occurred without any evidence of altered
prothrombin time, and (d) were not observed in all studies. This suggests that the
pharmacodynamic effect in healthy animals is nearly completely muted, presumably due to intact
coagulatory hemostatic regulatory mechanisms. Despite the absence of a clear does-response
relationship between marstacimab and lung emboli in healthy animals, a hypothetical risk of
clotting (e.g., lung or other emboli) in hemophilia patients given marstacimab cannot be
excluded.
A fertility and early embryofetal developmental (FEED) study was conducted in marstacimab
treated males mated with treatment naïve females. The study identified no adverse effects at
1000 mg/kg/week (i.e., the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL), which represented
exposure >200-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD). Based on results of the FEED study,
there is no evidence that marstacimab poses a risk to male fertility or the health of the resulting
embryo to the time of implantation. Marstacimab was not tested in reproduction studies in
female animals because hemophilia A and B occur almost exclusively in males. In the absence of
such data, the use of effective contraceptive measures by female patients with hemophilia should
be considered during treatment with marstacimab, and for five half-lives after the last dose.
In summary, despite the absence of a robust signal for emboli in healthy animals treated with
marstacimab, the hypothetical risk of emboli in patients with hemophilia cannot be excluded. No
adverse impact on male fertility or (male-mediated effect on) early embryonic development was
demonstrated.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Although healthy animals in toxicology studies did not identify any risk, based on marstacimab
mode of action, the risk of emboli in hemophilia patients cannot be ruled out. Section 7.6.3
discusses thromboembolic events during clinical studies and the risk of thromboembolic events
is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI based on class labeling.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Marstacimab is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or in any country.
63
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety assessment for the intended population of patients with HA or HB without inhibitor is
based on data derived from a total of 180 unique participants with HA or HB with or without
inhibitors who received at least one dose of marstacimab while enrolled in a clinical study
including the Phase 1b/2 B7841002/B7841003 studies, the Phase 3 B7841005 study, and the
long-term extension Study B7841007. A description of these studies are provided in Table 3. For
details on Study B7841005, see Section 6 and Section 15. None one of these studies included a
simultaneous comparator arm.
The primary safety profile for marstacimab is based on safety data from the active treatment
phase (ATP) of Study B7841005. As per study design, all participants regardless of treatment
received during the OP (OP-OD and OP-PPX) received marstacimab prophylaxis during the
ATP. There were 117 participants in these two cohorts who received at least one dose of
marstacimab; however, one of these participants is excluded from the data set due to termination
of the study site for Good Clinical Practice (GCP) noncompliance. This participant did not
experience any AEs. Therefore, the primary safety population (PSP) comprises 116 participants,
33 from the OP-OD cohort and 83 from the OP-PPX cohort.
The safety analysis of the PSP is based on a pooled population of the two cohorts because
whether participants received on-demand factor replacement or prophylaxis factor replacement
prior to enrollment or during the observation period is not expected to impact the safety profile
of marstacimab. Limited safety analyses for the PSP by cohort is provided in Section 17.2. This
analysis did not identify any significant differences in safety based on type of factor treatment
(on-demand or prophylaxis) received during the OP, and therefore, reporting in the USPI by
prior factor therapy (i.e., by cohort) is not indicated.
Based on the similarity of the underlying mechanism of HA and HB and the mechanism of
action of marstacimab, Study B7841005 did not enroll participants with HA and HB to separate
cohorts. For this same reason, a safety analysis of the PSP by hemophilia type is not expected to
be different in these two subpopulations. Thus, the primary safety analysis of the PSP is based on
participants with HA and HB. A limited safety analysis by hemophilia subtype is provided in
Section 17.2. The analysis did not identify any significant differences in safety based on type
hemophilia, and therefore, reporting in the USPI by hemophilia type is not indicated.
An analysis of a pooled marstacimab integrated safety population (ISP) was performed to
identify additional, rare safety signals that may become apparent only in a larger safety
population. The ISP comprises 179 unique participants with HA or HB with or without inhibitor
who received at least one dose of the marstacimab in Studies B7841005/B7841007 (n=151) and
B7841002/B7841003 (n=28). Of the 151 participants from Studies B7841005/B7841007, 116
were without inhibitors and 35 with inhibitors. Of the 28 participants from Studies
B7841002/B7841003, 19 were without inhibitors and 9 with inhibitors.
Three additional studies were reviewed individually for safety. This included six participants
with hemophilia A or B from Phase 1 study, Study B7841010, and 60 healthy participants from
Studies B7841009 and B7841001. Review of safety data from these participants did not identify
any potential safety signals not identified from analysis of the PSP or the ISP.
64
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
The safety assessment performed on data from the ongoing B7841005 study had a cutoff date of
April 17, 2023, for noninhibitors cohorts, and a cutoff date of March 10, 2023, for long term
B7841007 study. Studies B7841002, a Phase 1b/2, and B7841003 long term treatment were
completed. The 120-day safety update provided additional safety data collected from ongoing
clinical studies, including study ongoing B7841005, long-term study B7841007 and pediatric
Study B7841008, as of cutoff date of October 9, 2023.
Clinical study data were independently analyzed by the Agency. Clinical data scientists (CDSs)
from the Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables and figures. These
include safety tables and figures for participants disposition, exposure data, AEs, and serious
adverse events (SAEs). Additional exploration and analysis of AEs were performed by the
clinical reviewers using JMP, JMP Clinical, and the Office of Computational Science Medical
Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA)-based Adverse Event Diagnostics tool
(MAED). The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to the MedDRA (version 25.1)
preferred terms (PTs) for the events reported in Study B7841005 were reviewed and found to be
acceptable. The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that
precluded performing a thorough safety review.
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were analyzed at all levels of the MedDRA
hierarchy and using FDA Medical Queries (FMQ) narrow. FMQ (narrow) are a collection of
approximately 110 standardized groups, where each group comprises adverse event terms that
reflect a specific medical concept. Each FMQ term is composed of one or more PTs. FMQs are
not mutually exclusive, that is an individual PT may be counted in more than one FMQ. When
reporting FMQ analyses, all FMQs, including overlapping terms, are included in the presentation
of the data in this review. In addition, not all PTs are included in an FMQ. To assure that all
potential safety signals are identified, analysis of all reported TEAEs not included in at least one
FMQ were identified and analyzed. These PTs were generally laboratory assessments or vital
sign assessments.

of interest (AEIs)

In the ISP, the mean (SD) and median (Min, Max) marstacimab exposure were 447 (188) days
and 454 (7, 847), respectively. Among participants in the ISP, 89% were exposed to marstacimab
for 6 months or longer, and 78% were exposed for at least 1 year.
The size of the safety database is considered adequate for a comprehensive safety assessment of
marstacimab for the proposed indication, patient population, dosage regimen, and duration, given
the rarity of hemophilia. Although the analysis is based only single arm data, with no
comparator, this is consistent with other drug development programs for hemophilia.

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

7.6. Safety Results
Overall, marstacimab was well tolerated, with few participants having serious side effects or
requiring discontinuation or dose interruption, with a safety profile consistent with other
approved anti-hemophilic agents. Safety analysis showed marstacimab to have an acceptable
safety profile in both participants who received prior on-demand factor treatment or prophylactic
treatment with factors replacement. The design of the study was such that each participant served
as their own control during the observation period for efficacy but similar to other development
programs for hemophilia, the study design did not allow for randomized controlled safety data.
Key safety findings of adverse event analyses of the ATP in Study B7841005 are:
•

There were no fatal adverse events.

•

Serious adverse events (SAEs) occurred in 6% of participants. The most common SAE,
occurring in ≥2 participants, was hemorrhage which as considered likely related to
underlying disease and unlikely to be related to marstacimab based on its mechanism of
action (marstacimab is procoagulant agent) and disease of study.

•

Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of marstacimab were rare, occurring in
2 (2%) participants. One participant discontinued treatment due to atypical meningioma and
the other due to peripheral swelling.

•

Dose interruption of marstacimab occurred in 13% of participants. The most common AEs
leading to dose interruption, occurring in ≥2% of participants, was COVID-19 infection
(10%).

•

The most common adverse events, based on narrow FMQ, occurring in ≥5% of participants,
were viral infection (21%), local administration reactions (10%), hemorrhage (10%),
nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and pruritus (7%).

7.6.1. Safety Results, Active Treatment Phase, Study
B7841005
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Active Treatment Phase
Table 29 provides an overview of TEAEs, based on frequency analysis of the PSP receiving
marstacimab prophylaxis during the active treatment phase for Study B7841005.
During treatment with marstacimab, approximately two-thirds of participants experienced at
least one TEAE. Most TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity with only 5% of participants
having a severe AE. There were no AEs leading to death during the ATP. SAEs were reported in
7 (6%) participants. Permanent discontinuation due to TEAEs was reported in one participant,
due to a need for meningioma. Interruption due to TEAEs was reported for 15 (13%) of
participants, with most cases (12/15) due to COVID-19 infection.

Analysis by narrow FMQ did not identify any safety signal not captured in the analysis by
system organ class (SOC) and PT.
The SAEs of meningioma, tonsillitis, and tympanic membrane perforation were assessed by the
investigator to be unrelated to marstacimab. Based on the mechanism of action of marstacimab,
the review team concurs with this assessment.
The SAEs (PT level) of traumatic hemorrhage, hemorrhage, and hemarthrosis were considered
by the investigator as unrelated to study drug. The review team concurs with this assessment and
considered these events related to the underlying disease and unlikely to be related to treatment
with marstacimab, based on its procoagulant mechanism of action.
•

Participant
is a 21-year-old male with HA who experienced the SAE of chest pain.
On Day 159 of marstacimab, the participant experienced Grade 1 chest pain, which worsened
to Grade 2 on Day 167, and resulted in hospitalization for atypical chest pain, cough,
palpitation, and occasional dizziness. Extensive workup (including physical exam,
electrocardiogram (ECG), D-dimer and troponin, CT angiography of pulmonary arteries,
echocardiography, and MRI of brain with venogram) ruled out any cardiac, pulmonary, or
brain etiology. The chest pain resolved 5 days later without intervention. Marstacimab
treatment was not interrupted during the events. The Applicant assessed the event as not
related to marstacimab treatment or any clinical study procedures. The review team assessed
this SAE as unlikely to be related to marstacimab based on negative workup and resolution
without study drug interruption.

•

Participant
is a 31-year-old male with HA, with history of synovectomy 15 months
prior to start of marstacimab, who experienced right calf swelling on Day 24 of ATP
prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. The participant was hospitalized for the SAE of
peripheral swelling with an initial diagnosis of cellulitis and marstacimab held. A doppler
performed two days later showed no evidence of above the knee deep vein thrombosis
(DVT). The event was resolved by Day 26. The participant declined restarting treatment with
marstacimab and decided to withdraw from the study. The Applicant assessed this event as
related to marstacimab. Although the doppler did not show evidence of a DVT, because the
study was not performed until two days after hospitalization, the review team concluded it
could not rule out DVT and based on marstacimab mechanism of action assessed the event as
at least possibly related to marstacimab.

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation and Interruptions
Adverse events leading to permanent treatment discontinuation were uncommon during ATP,
occurring in only one participant (0.9%) who was diagnosed with an atypical meningioma and
(b) (6)
required surgery. Participant
, described above with right calf swelling, is not included
as having discontinued due to AE as the narrative indicated discontinuation of study treatment
was participant’s choice and the event was not coded by the investigator as leading to
discontinuation.
Dose reduction or interruption of marstacimab due to adverse events occurred in 13% of
participants. The most common, occurring in ≥2% of participants was COVID-19 infection
(10%). Drug discontinuation rates were low, supporting the tolerability of marstacimab.

marstacimab, based on PT. Overall, 65% of participants experienced at least one TEAE. The
most common TEAEs, occurring in ≥5 participants were COVID-19 (17%), headache (6%),
contusion (4%) and hypertension (4%).

Adverse Reactions
TEAEs occurring in ≥3% of participants during treatment with marstacimab in the ATP based on
FMQ (narrow) (Table 32), were viral infection (21%), local administration reaction (10%),
hemorrhage (10%), nasopharyngitis (9%), headache (7%) and pruritus (7%), systemic
hypertension (4%), and bacterial infection (3%).
The most common TEAEs based on PT, not subsumed by FMQ (narrow) events, are dental
carries (3%) and tonsillitis (3%) (Table 31).
The TEAEs of viral infection, nasopharyngitis, bacterial infection, dental carries, tonsillitis, and
hypertension were considered unlikely to be related to treatment with marstacimab based on the
mechanism of drug, and therefore were not considered to be adverse reactions.
The most common adverse reactions reported in ≥3% of participants and included in Section 6 of
the USPI, are injection site reactions (10%), headache (7%), and pruritus (3%). The frequency of
pruritis reported in the USPI excludes events reported with the PT injection site pruritis, which
are included in the event injection site reaction.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings
Routine monitoring of blood and urine, including complete blood count, clinical chemistries, and
hepatic and renal evaluations, were obtained regularly at prespecified points throughout the
study. This section presents a frequency-based analysis of laboratory assessments.
Chemistries
Frequency of abnormal laboratory values for chemistry assessments are shown in Table 33.
There were no significant changes in laboratory assessments for total protein, serum albumin,
blood urea nitrogen, sodium, calcium, potassium, chloride, alkaline phosphatase, alanine
aminotransferase, aspartate aminotransferase, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR, using
Modification of Diet in Renal Disease method for adult participants; eGFR using Ped Schwartz
method for adolescent participants), reported in during active treatment with marstacimab.
The majority of abnormal values for chemistry laboratory parameters were below level 2.
Abnormal values ≥ level 3 were not observed for any analyzed chemistries reported in the table.
Abnormal values above level 1 were rare, with the exception of low bicarbonate
level 15 to <18 mEq/L was observed in 17% of participants during ATP. The minimum bicarb
observed during the ATP across participants was 16.2 mEq/L. When evaluated at the participant
level, only two participants had more than a single assessment <18 mEq/L. Based on TEAE
reporting, neither of these participants had reasons for these lower bicarbonate values or
evidence of sequelae. The review team concluded that the lower bicarbonate values observed in
the participants were generally sporadic and not clinically significant.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury
Table 36 and Figure 11 show screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced
liver injury in Study B7841005. No potential cases met Hy’s Law criteria or are seen in
Temple’s Corollary quadrants of the hepatocellular scatterplots (eDISH) during ATP in Study
(b) (6)
B7841005. There was only one case in the cholestasis quadrant. This participant
had
evidence of cholestasis as manifested by maximum total bilirubin of 3.2 x ULN without elevated
liver enzymes. This participant had an elevated bilirubin at baseline and a diagnosis of Gilbert’s
syndrome.

Adverse Events in Subgroup of Patients Who Receive Dose Escalation to 300 mg QW
Marstacimab dose escalation from 150 mg SC QW to 300 mg SC QW was allowed after 6
months treatment with marstacimab during ATP Day 180 (Visit 14), if a participant met the
protocol-specified dose escalation criteria of weighing at least 50 kg and having two or more
spontaneous (atraumatic) bleeds of joint bleeds or significant soft tissue/muscle or other site
bleed, treated with infusion(s) of FVIII or FIX over a 6-month period in the absence of a
confirmed FVIII or FIX inhibitor.
In Study B7841005, a total of 14 participants had marstacimab dose escalation from 150 mg QW
to 300 mg QW. The mean (SD) exposure for the 14 participants who received 300 mg QW
marstacimab during ATP was 118.6 days (56.3), and the median exposure was 116.5 days.
In these participants, while receiving marstacimab 300 mg during ATP, there were no TEAEs
that led to discontinuation or SAEs. A total of 5 TEAEs were reported in 5 out of the 14
participants (36%) after escalation to the higher dosage of 300 mg QW:
•

Two participants experienced a TEAE of arthralgia on Study Day 190 and Day 252.

•

One participant experienced a TEAE of joint range of motion reduction on Study Day 183.

•

One participant experienced a TEAE of laryngitis on Study Day 205.

•

One participant experienced a TEAE of rhinitis on Study Day 191.

Overall, the frequency of TEAEs was 36% in participants when receiving a dosage of 300 mg
QW compared to 64% of all participants when receiving 150 mg QW. We note, however, that no
79
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
conclusion can be drawn about this observed difference as the study was not designed to
compare the safety of two doses, and the number of participants who required dose escalation
was small with shorter duration of exposure compared to the duration of exposure in most
participants receiving 150 mg QW dosage.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Integrated Safety Population
Assessment
To identify potential rare AEs that might be detectable only in a larger dataset, we performed an
analysis of the ISP, comprising 180 participants with HA or HB (see Section 7.4) Analysis of the
ISP considered only AEs occurring while participants were receiving marstacimab.
There were no deaths in any of the reviewed marstacimab studies.
The 120-day safety update was consistent with the original safety analysis.
Thromboembolic Events
There were no thromboembolic events in the ISP. There were no events of thrombotic
microangiopathy or disseminated intravascular coagulopathy/consumption coagulopathy in any
study in the marstacimab development program. There was one case of a thromboembolic event
that occurred in a healthy participant enrolled in a bioequivalence study. This case is discussed in
Section 7.6.3).
Drug Hypersensitivity
There were no reported cases of anaphylaxis, Stevens Johnson Syndrome, drug reaction with
eosinophilia and systemic symptoms, or toxic epidermal necrolysis in the ISP. There was one
TEAE event of urticaria that was incorrectly coded under PT "Anaphylaxis.” This event resolved
without sequelae after treatment with oral antihistamine and the participant continued treatment.
A total of 11 participants had reports at least an event of the preferred term hypersensitivity (see
Drug Hypersensitivity in Section 7.6.3 for more details).
Hy’s Law
The analysis performed on the ISP did not reveal any cases that met the criteria of Hy's Law.

7.6.3. Adverse Events of Special Interest
The Applicant identified the following adverse events of special interest (AESIs) based
mechanism of action, safety profiles of other drugs in same class, experience in early phase
clinical studies of marstacimab, events that may require additional monitoring or treatment
modification: injection site reactions, thromboembolic events, liver toxicity, and hemorrhage. In
addition, COVID-19 was designated as an AESI due to the potential to increase the risk of
thrombotic events. Table 39 summarizes the frequency of thromboembolic events, hemorrhage,
injection site reactions, hypersensitivity reaction, and hypertension.

Thromboembolic Events
Thromboembolic events were considered an AESI due the mechanism of action of the drug as a
thrombogenic agent and the increased risk of thromboembolic events observed with other antihemophilic drugs. Marstacimab has not been studied in patients with a history of previous
thromboembolic events (patients with history of thromboembolic events were excluded from
marstacimab studies).
During the marstacimab development program clinical studies, one healthy participant, who
received marstacimab while participating in the bioequivalence Phase 1 Study B7841009,
developed a thromboembolic event of pulmonary embolism and probable deep vein thrombosis
of the right posterior tibial vein 9 days after first receiving marstacimab and 29 days after
vaccination with the second dose of the AstraZeneca (ChAdOx1-S [recombinant] vaccine)
COVID-19 vaccine. The participant discontinued the study due to the event. The event resolved
and was considered related to marstacimab.
There were no participants with hemophilia across the development program who reported
thromboembolic events during prophylactic treatment with marstacimab. One participant
reported a Grade 2 (nonserious) hemorrhoid thrombosed which was considered by the
investigator as possibly related to marstacimab treatment; however, the event of hemorrhoid
thrombosed was attributed to local inflammation and not related to any thromboembolic event.
81
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
There were no thrombotic microangiopathy events reported in marstacimab development
program.
The risk of thromboembolic events is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI based
on class labeling. Due to the small size of the safety database and limited duration of exposure
for a drug that will be administered chronically, the Division recommended enhanced
pharmacovigilance for thromboembolic events be implemented at the time of approval.
Injection Site Reactions
Injection site reactions were considered an AESI based on the occurrence with drugs in the
related classes. In Study B7841005, injection site reaction was reported in 11 (10%) participants
during ATP prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. The majority of injection site reactions
were Grade 1 (10 participants). One participant reported 70 events of Grade 1 or 2 AEs
associated with injection, mostly as injection site pruritus, erythema, or induration. This
participant tested positive for neutralizing antibody (NAb) at Day 120. None of the injection site
reactions were serious or led to permanent discontinuation.
Injection site reactions were more frequent 9/26 (35%) in Study B7841002 where participants
received higher doses (150, 300, 450 mg) for 3 months. Most events were mild (six participants).
Severe injection site reactions were reported in two participants (one injection site pain, one
injection site swelling) who received 450 mg SC QW (1.5 mL injection volume).
Drug Hypersensitivity
In Study B7841005, hypersensitivity adverse events were reported in eight (6.9%) participants
during ATP prophylaxis treatment with marstacimab. None of these were severe or systemic
cases of hypersensitivity or anaphylaxis.
In the ISP analysis, three participants in Study B7841005 with hemophilia with inhibitors
reported events of stomatitis, rash, and anaphylactic reaction. One participant with HB with
inhibitor experienced a SAE of papular skin urticaria over the entire body (except head and
neck). The event was considered related to marstacimab, and the drug was discontinued. The
diagnosis was confirmed by three skin biopsies performed on three separate occasions, and the
participant was treated with systemic corticosteroids. A second participant experienced an
adverse event urticaria which was reported by the investigator as “Anaphylaxis” (Reported Term
for the Adverse Event [AETERM]). The participant was treated with one tablet (a single dose) of
loratadine and resolved without sequelae. The participant continued with their weekly dose of
150 mg marstacimab without further reaction. A third participant experienced an adverse event
of stomatitis which was considered related to marstacimab.
Hypersensitivity is included in Warnings and Precautions of the USPI.

7.6.4. QT-IRT Study Review
A thorough QT study was not conducted in humans. There were no clinically important changes
in ECGs observed in Study B7841005. No participants experienced postdose QTcF values
≥480 msec.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
No safety review issues were found.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Based on the population PK analysis, no clinically significant differences in the
pharmacokinetics of marstacimab were observed based on race (Asian or non-Asian), hemophilia
type (A and B), mild renal impairment (eGFR of 60 to 89 mL/min/1.73 m2), and hepatic function
as defined by total bilirubin 1× to ≤1.5× ULN.
Body weight was identified as a significant covariate impacting the pharmacokinetics of
marstacimab, and therefore was included as a structural covariate in the popPK final model (refer
to Section 14.5 for details). The popPK results showed that weight accounted for most of the
observed differences between adults and adolescents (i.e., the difference in clearance decreased
from 28.8% to 3.1% when accounted for body weight difference). Similarly, the difference of
clearance values decreased between healthy and hemophilia participants (19.9% to 12.1%) and
Asian and non-Asians (34.6% to 31.9%) when adjusted for body weight.
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal or hepatic impairment.
As marstacimab is a monoclonal antibody that is expected to be metabolized by catabolic
pathway and receptor-mediated clearance, the pharmacokinetic is not expected to be different in
participants with renal or hepatic impairment.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
No clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) study was conducted with marstacimab. As marstacimab
is a monoclonal antibody, PK-based DDI is unlikely to occur.
In vitro DDI studies were performed with marstacimab and bypass agents such as recombinant
Factor VIIa (rFVIIa) and aPCC in plasma of participants with hemophilia using thrombin
generation assay (TGA). The studies showed that there was no additive effect when marstacimab
was used in combination with rFVIIa. While thrombin level increased when marstacimab was
used in combination with aPCC compared to marstacimab alone, it was still within the range of
marstacimab used in nonhemophilic plasma, which would have full complement of coagulation

83
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
factors. Therefore, bypassing agents can be used in cases of breakthrough bleeds while patients
are on marstacimab.

Animal Data
Dedicated juvenile animal studies have not been conducted with marstacimab.

8.1 Pregnancy
Pregnancy: Reproduction studies have not been conducted in female animals
based on target patient population being limited to male subjects. Hemophilia A
and B are X-linked inherited blood clotting disorder which occurs predominantly
in males and is extremely rare in females. Since the risk to female patients is
minimal but not absent, recommendation to include the use of contraception is
advisable.

male rats mated with naïve females, doses up to 1000 mg/kg/week had no effect on
any of the reproductive parameters (volume of the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility
or sperm morphology). Treated males successfully mated with untreated female rats.

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed BLA 761369 with respect to
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards
to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, the Office of
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this BLA from a quality perspective.

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
Marstacimab is provided as a 150 mg/mL solution for injection in either a single-dose prefilled
syringe (PFS) or prefilled pen (PFP) for single use for subcutaneous administration. Each
prefilled syringe or pen are designed to deliver 150 mg of marstacimab-hncq in 1 mL of solution.
Marstacimab is supplied in carton that contains either one single-dose Type I glass prefilled
syringe with a plunger stopper (chlorobutyl elastomer) or one single-dose prefilled pen with
glass syringe inside the pen with needle guard. In addition, each carton contains stainless steel 27
gauge, ½ inch staked needle with a rigid needle shield (thermoplastic elastomer).
The Center for Devices and Radiological Health was consulted for evaluation of this product and
found the product approvable.

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
Study B7841005 was conducted in accordance with the principles of the Declaration of Helsinki
Council, and International Conference on Harmonization GCP guidelines. An Independent
Ethics Committee and/or Institutional Review Board reviewed approved and monitored the
study. Signed written informed consent forms by participants or their legally authorized
representative were required for study enrollment. A Clinical Site Inspection consult review was
completed by Dr. Anthony Orencia in the Office of Scientific Investigations and is summarized
below in Section 22. A Financial Disclosure summary review is located in Section 25.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
This application did not raise efficacy or safety issues requiring external expert advice.
Therefore, an Advisory Committee meeting was not convened to discuss this application.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Marstacimab is a human monoclonal antibody directed against tissue factor pathway inhibitor
(TFPI) that has been developed for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of
bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A
(HA) (congenital factor VIII (FVIII) deficiency) without factor VIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B
(HB) (congenital factor IX (FIX) deficiency) without factor IX inhibitors.
Marstacimab was granted orphan drug designation on May 18, 2016.
On November 18, 2016, FDA received a submission for a new IND application under Section
505(i) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act (IND 126734). Prior to the IND submission,
three meetings were held with the Applicant:
•

Type B pre-IND meeting was held on August 6, 2015, to discuss the overall nonclinical and
clinical development plan for marsticimab,

•

Type C Written Responses were issued on May 11, 2016, to discuss the nonclinical
development plan, and

•

Type C Written Responses were issued on September 20, 2016, to discuss the
appropriateness of design of the phase 1b/2 study.

On December 16, 2016, the Applicant was advised that the 30-day, safety review of the
application had concluded and that the study may proceed with their proposed clinical
investigation for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in
adults and children with hemophilia A or hemophilia B (with or without inhibitors).
Marstacimab was submitted for fast-track review twice. The first submission on May 15, 2017,
was denied due to an inability to determine, based on the preclinical data provided and lack of
clinical data, if marstacimab had the potential to meet an unmet medical need in patients with
hemophilia who have inhibitors. The second review was submitted on July 19, 2019, for which
the Applicant was granted fast track designation for marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to
prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in hemophilia A with inhibitors or
hemophilia B with inhibitors on September 17, 2019.
On September 21, 2017, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed clinical
program to support initial registration of marstacimab for prophylaxis use in patients with
hemophilia A and hemophilia B with inhibitors, and a subsequent submission to support
prophylaxis use in patients with hemophilia A and hemophilia B patients without inhibitors.
On February 14, 2018, the Applicant requested a Type C meeting to discuss the quality and
clinical proposed drug product development strategy for the commercial drug product (DP)
presentations, including both prefilled syringe (PFS) and prefilled pen (PFP). This meeting
request was subsequently withdrawn by the Applicant after receiving the FDA’s preliminary
86
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
comments on May 23, 2018, stating that the Applicant planned to submit a Type C (Written
Response Only) meeting request to gain further clarification in writing, relative to the FDA’s
preliminary comments and the acceptability of the Applicant’s approach to implement Agency
guidance.
On August 2, 2018, a Type C guidance meeting was held to seek agreement on key outstanding
clinical development items from the September 2017, Type C meeting and to inform plans for a
subsequent Type C meeting planned with FDA for October 2018 that would seek Agency
endorsement of the full draft phase 3 pivotal study protocol. The FDA advised the Applicant
during this meeting that there were concerns with the following:
•

The proposal to switch patients without inhibitors who were on stable prophylactic regimens
to marstacimab.

•

The proposed number of subjects with HB was the minimum number of subjects needed to
potentially support an indication and was likely not sufficient to adequately assess the safety
and efficacy, given the potential for patient discontinuations and dropouts.

•

The phase 3 protocol should specify the number of adolescent subjects (ages 12 to 18) to be
enrolled and that a sufficient number of adolescent subjects would need to be enrolled to
support an indication.

On August 15, 2018, written responses were issued to the Applicant to provide further
clarification on the Type C meeting preliminary comments sent by the FDA on May 23, 2018, in
regard to the quality and clinical proposals on the drug product development strategy for the
commercial drug product (DP) presentations, including both PFS and PFP. The FDA informed
the Applicant that their approach to product quality testing in PFS and PFP was not appropriate
because they provided insufficient description of the planed characterization studies and testing
plan. Further, the Applicant was informed that they needed to submit the Human Factors
Validation study protocol for the 150 mg PFP presentation for Agency to review.
On October 23, 2018, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed Phase 3
pivotal study design, within the context of the described clinical development plan, in follow-up
to the August 2, 2018, Type C meeting. The Applicant was informed during this meeting that the
FDA had concerns with switching patients without inhibitors who are on stable prophylactic
regimens to marstacimab.
On October 29, 2018, final written responses were issued to the Applicant to reach agreement
with the FDA on key aspects of the quality and nonclinical development strategy. A key
agreement made during this meeting was that the carcinogenic risk of marstacimab appeared low
and carcinogenicity studies were not warranted at that time. However, it was recommended that
the carcinogenicity assessment be submitted with the proposed BLA.
On March 5, 2020, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the additional data provided
from the completion of the phase 2 study, Study B7841002, and the interim analysis of the 12month phase 2 extension study, Study B7841003 and whether or not the findings were sufficient
to allow the inclusion of hemophilia A or B patients previously on stable factor prophylaxis
therapy into the phase 3 study, B7841005. The FDA agreed that patients previously on stable
87

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
prophylaxis could be included on the study but recommended that the Applicant submit the
phase 3 protocol for review.
On December 6, 2021, a Type C guidance meeting was held to discuss the proposed statistical
analysis plan and BLA submission strategy. The FDA expressed concerns with submitting only
interim data analysis including but not limited to separate BLAs.
On May 9, 2022, the Applicant submitted a Type C meeting request to discuss the aspects of a
planned BLA submission. This meeting request was cancelled by the Applicant after receiving
the FDA’s preliminary comments on July 20, 2022, stating the responses provided by the FDA
were thorough and no further follow up was needed.
On November 1, 2022, Written Responses were issued to the Applicant to discuss chemistry,
manufacturing, and controls specific questions in preparation for the BLA submission for
licensure of marstacimab.
On June 21, 2023, a Type B, pre-BLA meeting request was held to discuss the proposed
submission of a BLA for marstacimab. The Applicant was informed that it was unclear if the
clinical package would be sufficient to support the submission of a BLA as the Applicant had not
described a plan to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE).
On September 5, 2023, the Applicant submitted a request for Breakthrough Therapy Designation
Request Advice for the marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency
of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 6 months of age and older with: hemophilia
A (congenital FVIII deficiency) with or without FVIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (congenital
FIX deficiency) with or without FIX inhibitors. The FDA informed the Applicant that there was
insufficient information includes with the request for advice to determine if it was appropriate for
the Applicant to submit breakthrough therapy designation request.
On October 5, 2024, the Applicant submitted a Breakthrough Therapy Designation Request for
marstacimab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in
adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (congenital FVIII
deficiency) without FVIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (congenital FIX deficiency), without FIX
inhibitors. The request was denied due to lack of clinical evidence indicating that marstacimab
prophylaxis may demonstrate substantial improvements over existing therapies on one or more
clinically significant endpoints.
FDA received BLA 761369 under Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act on October
11, 2023. The Applicant requested priority review. The application was accepted with no filing
review issues. The application was set to be reviewed under a standard review timeline. The goal
date for this application is October 11, 2024.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
13.1.1. Pharmacology (Primary and Secondary)
Tissue factor (TF) is a procoagulant protein located outside the vasculature that is released upon
tissue injury to prevent excessive hemorrhage. Tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) is a plasma
serine protease inhibitor of TF. Marstacimab (PF-06741086) is a fully humanized
immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) monoclonal antibody that targets the K2 domain of TFPI and
reduces TFPI activity (see Figure 12). By blocking TFPI, marstacimab enhances TF activity and
promotes the extrinsic coagulation process in participants with hemophilia A and B (i.e., those
with genetic deficiency in the intrinsic coagulation pathway, in response to damage to the
endothelial wall of blood vessels). Given that TF and TFPI are released outside the vasculature in
response to injury, they are considered part of the extrinsic coagulation pathway. Both the
extrinsic and intrinsic pathways converge on a common pathway beginning with the activation of
factor X.
Figure 12. Schematic Pathway in the Coagulation Cascade and the Role of Marstacimab in the
Extrinsic Pathway

Source: Modified figure of the Applicant and Crawley and Lane (2008)
Abbreviations: FVIIa, factor VIIa; FXa, factor Xa; K1, Kunitz domain 1; K2, Kunitz domain 2; K3, Kunitz domain 3; TF, tissue factor;
TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor

Inhibition and neutralization of TFPI in the extrinsic pathway by marstacimab is thought to
reduce the negative feedback on FVIIa/TF/Factor X (FXa) complex, promoting free FXa to enter
the common coagulation pathway to promote clot formation. By enhancing free FXa in a manner
independent of the intrinsic pathway, marstacimab can provide an alternative treatment option
for patients with both HA (FVIII deficiency) and HB (FIX deficiency).
89
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Marstacimab binds to TFPI at an epitope on the Kunitz-type domain K2 with similar affinity to
the combined K1K2 domains (via K2), with a Kd of 2.63 and 3.7nM, respectively
(Study #072904). Marstacimab has similar binding affinities to human, monkey, rabbit, rat, and
mouse combined K1K2 domains (Table 41). Binding affinity of marstacimab to TFPI K2 of the
combined K1K2 domains relative to concizumab). The triple Fc mutations caused marstacimab
to have similar or lower binding to FcɣR than an isotype control IgG1 monoclonal antibody
(mAb) lacking effector function, suggesting that marstacimab also has negligible effector
function (Study #15GR003).
TFPI is an endogenous serine protease inhibitor with at least three known Kunitz-type domains
(K1, K2 and K3) separated by a short stretch of amino acids. TFPI is expressed by endothelial
cells in α and β isoforms generated by alternative splicing of its pre-messenger RNA (Huntington
and Baglin 2018). Mechanistic studies show marstacimab binds to the K2 domain of TFPI
(Study #072904 & 121926). The enhanced coagulatory activity of marstacimab is based on
binding to TFPI K2. By binding to TFPI K2, marstacimab prevents inactivation of both FXa and
FVIIa, enhancing the extrinsic coagulation pathway, promoting hemostasis, and minimizing the
need for FVIII or FIX. A comparative study of marstacimab to concizumab showed that
concizumab is a nearly 7-fold more potent inhibitor of TFPI K2 in humans than concizumab. The
binding affinity of marstacimab and concizumab to the combined K1K2 domains of TFPI is
shown in Table 41. Based on binding affinity, the Applicant predicted a clinically effective dose
of between 100 to 300 mg marstacimab.

In vitro studies show marstacimab can neutralize the K2 (half-maximal effective concentration
[EC50] of 1312 ng/mL or 8.75nM, marked by changes in FXa), and the combined TFPI K1K2
(EC50=1985 ng/mL or 13.23nM) domains of TFPI in a dose-dependent manner relative to
isotype control mAb (Figure 13).

Marstacimab was effective in neutralizing TFPI activity in vitro in normal whole human blood,
suggesting that it is likely to be effective in inhibiting TFPI (platelets and plasma) under normal
coagulation conditions. There was a marstacimab-associated dose-dependent decrease in clotting
time and clot formation time relative to an isotype control IgG1 mAb. The clot strength was not
affected. The greatest activity was achieved at concentrations ≥16 µg/mL marstacimab (Study
#142056).
Platelet-poor plasma was generated by addition of citrate to plasma from healthy human
volunteers, rabbits, and cynomolgus monkeys (Study #123404). Addition of marstacimab to
nonhemophilic platelet-poor human plasma resulted in a dose-dependent decrease in diluted
prothrombin time (dPT) (EC50=406 ng/mL). There was no change in dPT in the isotype control
group (IgG1 or phosphate buffered saline). Neither marstacimab nor the control treatment had
any effect on activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) (Figure 14). A dose-dependent
decrease in dPT clotting time was also observed in platelet-poor plasma of rabbits
(EC50=629 ng/mL) and monkeys (EC50=273 ng/mL).

Figure 17).

Consistent with the intended pharmacology, there was minimal change in aPTT (i.e., no change
in this measure of the intrinsic pathway). Marstacimab restored thrombin in citrated platelet-poor
HA with inhibitor or HB plasma (Figure 18). The findings suggest marstacimab may be capable
of restoring thrombin in both hemophilia A and B patients. Marstacimab was also effective in
generating thrombin compared to recombinant FVIIa (Eptacog alpha up to 20 µg/mL) and a
combination (marstacimab+rFVII).

to nonhemophilic plasma, which suggests that marstacimab may improve thrombin levels in
patients with hemophilia A and B.

Marstacimab Activity In Vivo
The effectiveness of marstacimab was tested using in vivo models of HA (factor VIII deficient
mice) and HB (Factor IX deficient mice). A laser induced injury was used to cremaster arteriolar
endothelium to initiate injury (Study #135244 and #121321). The pharmacological effects of
marstacimab on bleeding parameters (e.g., platelets, fibrin formation) were also assessed
following laser injury in HA mice. Marstacimab decreased blood loss in both HA (76%) and HA
(65%) mouse models. There were dose-dependent increases in coagulation in both HA and HB
mouse models. Marstacimab increased platelet accumulation and fibrin generation in
precapillary arterioles in HA mice. Marstacimab administered after onset of bleeding restored
hemostasis within 2 minutes in the HB mouse tail clip injury model. Marstacimab’s effect on
reducing blood loss in this model lasted up to 192 hours (Figure 4).

In Vivo Pharmacology Studies Conducted With
Marstacimab
Pharmacological activity of marstacimab was examined in hemophilia A and B mouse models.
Marstacimab was effective in enhancing coagulation and bleeding in these models, supporting
the proposed prophylactic benefits of marstacimab. The summaries of these studies are shown in
Table 43 and Table 44.

13.1.2.1. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology assessments were incorporated into the 3-month toxicology study in
monkeys (Table 49). Intravenous administration of marstacimab at doses ranging from 30 to
500 mg/kg/week and SC administration of 90 mg/kg/week to monkeys had no effect on
respiratory, neurological, or cardiovascular parameters. There was no change in QTc interval
following intravenous administration of 500 mg/kg/week marstacimab in monkeys.

13.1.2.1.1. ADME/Pharmacokinetics
The pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicokinetics (TK) of marstacimab were characterized
following once weekly subcutaneous and intravenous administrations in both Wistar Han rats
and cynomolgus monkeys. Single and repeat dose PK (Study #13MA070, 8299672) and repeat
dose TK studies (Studies #20064198, #20062114, #20089324) showed marstacimab exposure
increased with increases in dose in both rats and monkeys. Importantly, marstacimab is cleared
by target-mediated drug disposition. Low doses are rapidly cleared by target, while higher doses
saturate target-mediated clearance, leading increasing half-life (t1/2). The t1/2 for marstacimab at
1 mg/kg intravenous (IV) was 12.3 hours in cynomolgus monkeys but was extended to 118 hours
at 10 mg/kg IV (Table 50 and Table 51). The volume of distribution (Vd) in the monkey was
estimated to be 34 to 65 mL/kg, which is similar the Vd of other monoclonal antibodies.

PK/PD, pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic; T1/2, elimination half-life; TK, toxicokinetic; Vss, volume of distribution at steady-state

Administration of marstacimab in combination with other compounds had no impact on
marstacimab levels in rats suggesting drug-drug interaction is highly unlikely. No specific
metabolism, protein binding, or excretion studies were conducted in nonclinical species.
Marstacimab is not expected to bind to albumin or α-1 acid glycoprotein. Marstacimab is
predicted to be metabolized by a proteomic catabolism. Clearance of marstacimab follows a
target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD), which predicts that higher affinity will result in
higher clearance. Since marstacimab has lower affinity to TFPI than concizumab, larger doses
should be necessary to produce a similar pharmacodynamic (PD) effect as concizumab. TMDD
theory also predicts that lower doses will be cleared more rapidly while larger saturating doses
will lead to a longer half-life. The low potency of marstacimab relative to concizumab (7-fold
lower) thus requires a larger dose to achieve therapeutic efficacy, which results in a longer halflife that allows less frequent dosing of marstacimab in both nonclinical species (once weekly)
and humans (once weekly mg) compared to concizumab.

13.1.2.2. Toxicokinetic Parameters
TK parameters with administration of marstacimab by the SC and IV routes to Wistar Han rats
and Cynomolgus monkey are shown in Table 52, Table 53, and Table 54.
Plasma levels of marstacimab in nonclinical species were measured using a validated biotinlabeled mouse anti-marstacimab monoclonal antibody on streptavidin coated microtiter plate and
detected using a ruthenium-labeled mouse anti-human IgG Fc antibody. Analysis of plasma
samples for antidrug antibody (ADA) detected ADA in a minority of rats (~28%) in the 3-month
study found no evidence of marstacimab neutralization (Table 52). There was no evidence of
ADA in the 3-month monkey or 6-month rat studies. Marstacimab’s systemic exposure increased
with dose in a slightly greater than dose-proportional manner on Days 29 and 85 in the 3-month
rat study but in a dose-proportional manner in the 3-month monkey and 6-month rat studies.
There were no sex-related differences in drug exposures in rats or monkeys. Following repeated
SC dosing of marstacimab, time to maximum plasma concentration (Tmax) ranged between 48 to
63 hours in rats and from 41 to 64 hours in monkeys.

13.1.2.2.1. General Toxicology
Marstacimab produced a clear pharmacodynamic response in mouse hemophilia models. While
marstacimab increased D-dimer, a marker of blood clot formation, in healthy rats and monkeys,
administration of marstacimab did not decrease prothrombin time, APTT, or increase fibrin. No
histopathological evidence of bleeding was observed at 200-times the area under the
concentration-time curve (AUC) at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) in the 3month rat study (doses up to 1000 mg/kg/week) or 3-month monkey study (doses up to
500 mg/kg/week). However, minimal acute emboli were detected in the 6-month rat study in the
lung at 60 (1F, 1M), 180 (4F, 2M) and 1000 mg/kg/week (1F, 0M) doses and in tail injection
sites at 180 (2F, 1M) and 1000 mg/kg/week (2F, 1M). Neither the lung nor the tail injection site
thrombi/emboli findings in the 6-month rat study were considered adverse based on (a) absence
of secondary lung tissue injury, (b) lack of clear dose-response, and (c) absence of change in
prothrombin time, a marker of extrinsic and common pathway integrity. Similar acute minimal
incidences of lung emboli were observed at the low dose of 30 mg/kg/week in one monkey (3month study) and in one male rat (pharmacology study) at 50 mg/kg. In none of the cases was
there any evidence of secondary lung injury, suggesting the lung emboli likely occurred at a very
low level or at the study termination, possibly due to combined effect of marstacimab plus stress
(exsanguination). Despite the unclear relationship between lung emboli in healthy animals and
marstacimab, the anticipated risk of thrombi/emboli in hemophilia patients cannot be dismissed.

105
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
In summary, the presence of marstacimab-related acute minimal lung and tail thrombi/emboli
identified sporadically across studies, suggests that marstacimab contributes to a minimally procoagulatory state in healthy animals that remains in homeostasis under most conditions. Thus,
the top weekly intravenous dose of 1000 mg/kg/dose in rats and 500 mg/kg/dose in monkeys
were regarded as the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) doses, with AUC exposures
approximately 200-times greater than clinical exposure at the maximum recommended human
dose (Table 55).

Study #20064198 / Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and
Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Phase
Key Study Findings
•

Minimal, nondose dependent decreases in prothrombin time, with an unexpected
prolongation of APTT, and lower fibrin concentrations, were observed during the study but
not at the end of the recovery.

•

Marstacimab was not associated any adverse histopathology findings in rats at SC doses up
to 180 mg/kg/week or IV doses up to 1000 mg/kg/wk.

•

ADAs were detected in 9/32 (28.1%) treated animals but had no impact on marstacimab
exposure.

•

With no marstacimab related adverse findings, the highest 1000 mg/kg/week IV and
180 mg/kg/week SC doses were accepted as the NOAELs in the 3-month rat study. Exposure
at the SC NOAEL dose were 5-times the exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis.

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Study #20062114 / Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and
Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Cynomolgus Monkeys With a 6-Week Recovery
Phase
Key Study Findings
•

Contrary to expected pharmacodynamic effect of marstacimab, there was an increase in
prothrombin time and aPTT and decrease in fibrinogen concentration (FIB) concentrations
(~60%). There was, however, a significant increase in D-dimer concentration (DDIM)
(marker of blood clot) that supported pharmacological activity.

•

There was a single minimal acute incidence of lung emboli at low dose of 30 mg/kg/week
IV.

•

There were no detectable ADAs in marstacimab treated monkeys given by IV or SC route.

•

The highest doses of 500 mg/kg/wk IV and 90 mg/kg/week SC were considered the NOAEL
doses. Exposures at the IV NOAEL were approximately 219X the exposure at the MRHD
dose on an AUC basis. Exposures at the highest SC dose of 90 mg/kg were roughly 40X the
exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis.

Study #20089324 / Title: A 6-Month Intravenous Bolus Injection Toxicity Study of PF06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Period
Key Study Findings
•

Contrary to expected pharmacodynamic effect, marstacimab increased prothrombin time at
HD of 1000 mg/kg/week in both sexes and increased APTT (all doses) and decreased FIB
(all doses). DDIM levels, a marker of increase in risk of blood-clot formation was increased
at all doses.

•

Minimal acute thrombi/emboli with no secondary tissue injury was observed in the lungs and
tail vein injection sites at ≥60 mg/kg/week.

•

In contrast to the results of the 3-month rat study, there were no detectable ADAs in the 6month rat study. Based on the minimal emboli being considered non-adverse, the highest IV
dose of 1000 mg/kg/week was considered the NOAEL in rats (approximately 200X the
exposure at the MRHD on an AUC basis).
13.1.2.2.2. Genetic Toxicology

As a monoclonal antibody, genotoxicity assessment of marstacimab was not scientifically
appropriate and therefore was not conducted.

107
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
13.1.2.2.3. Carcinogenicity
As a biological compound, the genotoxicity of marstacimab was evaluated based on a weight of
evidence (WoE) approach, the key points of which are highlighted below:
•

Biological nature of marstacimab: Biologicals possess no inherent carcinogenic risk based on
chemical structure or metabolism.

•

Mechanism of action/therapeutic target: Marstacimab is an inhibitor of TFPI, a blood clotting
regulator with no identified role in carcinogenic pathways.

•

Toxicology data: No preneoplastic lesions, evidence of hormonal perturbation, or signs of
immune modulation were observed across studies in animals.

Based on the WoE provided by the Applicant, the Division agreed that marstacimab is unlikely
to pose a significant carcinogenicity safety concern for humans and therefore considered a 2-year
study in rats unlikely to provide additional useful information.
13.1.2.2.4. Reproductive Toxicology
Study #00655204 / Title: A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of PF06741086 by Intravenous Injection in Male Wistar Rats
Key Study Findings
•

Males Wistar rats dosed intravenously once weekly with marstacimab up to 1000 mg/kg
(total of 11 doses) showed no adverse effects on fertility.

•

Marstacimab treatment in males mated with treatment-naïve females did not affect
pregnancy, implantation, or embryonic survival.

•

The highest once weekly dose of 1000 mg/kg marstacimab was accepted as the NOAEL.
Although drug exposures were not determined, plasma concentrations of marstacimab
measured at 5 minutes postdose supported a determination of adequate drug exposure,
because values were consistent with exiting TK data. Based on existing AUC data, exposure
at the NOAEL in the study was likely 201-times the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg per
week on an AUC basis.

As a biological compound, marstacimab impurities consisted of process-related impurities,
product-related impurities, and potential contaminant in the drug substance. Process-related
impurities originated from manufacturing process which consisted of host cell proteins, host cell
DNA and cell culture media components and impurities related to downstream processing of
marstacimab. The residual DNA impurities were DNA fragments (CHO cell line), cell culture

Excipients in Drug Product
Marstacimab is an IgG1 lambda human mAb with two identical heavy chains and two identical
light chains, covalently linked with four inter-chain disulfide bonds. To be marketed
marstacimab is a sterile, preservative free and colorless drug product (150 mg/mL, pH 5.8)
formulated for subcutaneous injection in a 1 mL prefilled single dose disposable glass (Type I
(b) (4)
glass) syringe pen with a 27-gauge thin walled 1/2-inch needle with a chlorobutyl
elastomer plunger stopper.
Excipients used in marstacimab clinical formulation are all generally regarded as safe (Lhistidine and L-histidine monohydrochloride, sucrose, disodium edetate, polysorbate 80 (Table
58). Marstacimab is sensitive to light therefore susceptible to photodegradation. All excipients
had been used at similar or higher concentrations with other injectables (Table 59).

Leachability Study
The leachability study designed to identify the daily exposure to leachable compounds from the
container (glass syringe and chlorobutyl plunger stopper) was based on notable compounds
detected in the extractability study. The safety concern threshold (SCT) defined as 1.5 µg/day
TDI for each mutagenic and 5 µg/day for non-mutagenic compounds per International Council
for Harmonisation M7(R1) was applied to safety assessment of leachable compounds. Any
compound exceeding 1.5 µg/day was identified and quantitated. Leachability studies have been
completed for up to 12 months and long-term studies up to 60 months are ongoing. All organic
compounds in the 6-month leachability tests were below the quantification limit with the
(b) (4)
(b)
exception of
at (4) µg/day (Table 62). Since all TDIs were lower than PDE
(International Council for Harmonisation Q3C) and have established toxicological data by all
routes, the Applicant did not carry any further risk assessment. However, the chemistry,
(b) (4)
manufacturing, and controls reviewer considered
as potential concern and
requested further safety assessment.
