Conclusion
In summary, compounds identified in the leachability studies coming from glass syringe and
chlorobutyl plunger stopper were within the acceptable daily limits and do not pose a safety risk
to humans. It should be noted that both the glass barrel pen and chlorobutyl plunger stopper are
obtained from commercials sources and have been used with similar products.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
Study #200064198 /Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and
Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Phase
Key Study Findings
•

Minimal and nondose dependent decrease in prothrombin time, prolongation of APTT (at all
doses), and lower FIB concentrations were observed during the study but not at the end of the
recovery.

•

There were no marstacimab associated adverse histopathology findings in rats.

•

Non-neutralizing ADAs were detected in 9/32 (28.1%) treated animals (no change in drug
exposure).

•

No marstacimab related adverse findings were seen in rats. The top doses of 1000 mg/kg
once weekly IV and 180 mg/kg once weekly SC were considered NOAELs in rats. Exposure
at the NOAEL dose was 212X the exposure at the MRHD on AUC basis.

Study #0062114/Title: A 3-Month Intravenous or Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity and
Toxicokinetic Study of PF-06741086 in Cynomolgus Monkeys With a 6-Week Recovery
Phase
Key Study Findings
•

Contradictory to marstacimab PD, there was no change in prothrombin time and aPTT and
decrease in FIB concentrations (~60%). Marstacimab treated monkeys however had
significant increase in DDIM (marker of blood clot) supported some marstacimab-related
pharmacological activity in monkeys.

•

The single minimal acute incidence of lung emboli at low dose of 30 mg/kg/week IV was not
considered marstacimab-related based on the lack of similar findings in other animals at any
dose levels and lack of similar findings in other tissues.

•

There were no detectable ADAs in marstacimab treated monkeys given by IV or SC route.

•

There were no adverse histopathology findings in monkeys. The top doses of 500 mg/kg/wk
IV and 90 mg/kg/wk SC were considered NOAELs. Exposure at the IV NOAEL was about

Study #20089324 /Title: A 6-Month Intravenous Bolus Injection Toxicity Study of PF06741086 in Rats With a 6-Week Recovery Period
Key Study Findings
•

Marstacimab increased prothrombin time at 1000 mg/kg/wk in both sexes and contrary to
pro-coagulatory effect, marstacimab increased APTT and DDIM and decreased FIB at all
doses in both sexes

•

Minimal Acute marstacimab-related evidence of thrombi/emboli with no secondary tissue
injury was observed the lungs and tail vein injection sites at ≥60 mg/kg/wk.

•

In contrast to the 3-month rat study, there were no detectable ADA in the 6-month rat study.
Based on minimal nonadverse findings, the top weekly IV dose of 1000 mg/kg
(AUC168h =1,840,000 µg.h/mL) was considered the NOAELs in rats and the exposure was
201X the exposure at the MRHD on AUC basis (AUC168h 9146 µg.h/mL).

Histopathology
Adequate battery:
Yes

No marstacimab-related effect
No marstacimab-related effect. However, dark focused discolorations were noted in
the thymus among all groups including control. They correlated with acute
hemorrhage observed microscopically. Since they were observed also in control,
the relation to marstacimab is not clear and likely were associated to old age.
No marstacimab-related effect
Histopathology findings were observed in the lung and tail vein injection site.
The lung findings included acute minimal emboli in males (C:0, LD:2, MD3, HD:1)
and females (C:0, LD:1, MD:0, HD:4), minimal cellular infiltrate with refractile
material in the lung, and minimal basophilic or nonbasophilic (light gray or clear)
foreign material in the lung at ≥60 mg/kg/dose in males and/or females. There were
no such findings in the 3m rat or monkeys treated with marstacimab doses up to
1000 mg/kg/wk.
In the tail vain, the minimal thrombi/emboli (acute and organizing) were observed at
the injection site at ≥180 mg/kg/dose in males and at ≥60 mg/kg/dose in females.
Acute minimal thrombi/emboli at the injection site were characterized by pale
staining eosinophilic granular to fibrillar material within the very small vessel.
Although thrombi/emboli are common at venipuncture sites; the minimal increase in
incidence in the emboli in lungs compared with controls appear to correspond with
the expected drug pharmacology, however, in absence of secondary effect at the
site of emboli and increase in PT and aPTT, the relation to marstacimab is not
clear. Based on minimal incidence and acute nature of emboli in some treated
animals, it is highly possible that emboli were triggered during the last stage of the
study, i.e., euthanasia/exsanguination.

13.2.1. Reproductive Toxicology
Study #00655204 / Title: A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of PF06741086 by Intravenous Injection in Male Wistar Rats
Key Study Findings
•

Marstacimab treated males up to 1000 mg/kg/wk, mated with native females did not affect
male reproductive performance or spermatogenesis neither pregnancy, implantation, nor
embryonic survival in untreated pregnant females.

•

Plasma concentration at 5 min post 1000 mg/kg in male rats (5,0800,000 ng/mL) was
consistent with drug exposure in toxicity studies, thus AUC for exposure multiple was based
on 6-month rat study. Based on existing AUC data, the exposure in males was estimated to
be 212X the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg on AUC basis.

•

The top dose of 1000 mg/kg/wk was accepted as NOAEL. Exposure at the NOAEL dose was
201X the exposure at the MRHD of 300 mg per week on AUC basis.

Good Laboratory Practice compliance: Yes

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
In patients with hemophilia who develop inhibitors4, the Applicant proposes that bypass agents5,
such as recombinant Factor VIIa (rFVIIa) or activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC)
may be used to treat breakthrough bleeds while on marstacimab prophylaxis. Therefore, the
Applicant conducted in vitro drug-drug interaction studies to evaluate the potential additive
effect of marstacimab and the bypass agents on coagulation parameters.
Marstacimab When Used in Combination With rFVIIa, Study #122443
The additive effect of marstacimab and rFVIIa on thrombin generation in hemophilic plasma
with or without inhibitors was evaluated using the TGA. The peak thrombin levels when rFVIIa
and marstacimab were administered alone or in combination in plasma of patients with HA with
or without inhibitor, and HB without inhibitor are provided in Table 78. Marstacimab
concentration of 16 µg/mL was selected to approximate steady state maximum plasma
concentration (Cmax) based on modeling of 2 mg/kg subcutaneous dose. The Cmax,ss based on the
population PK modeling of 150 mg SC weekly dose (with loading dose of 300 mg) is
19.7 µg/mL. The peak thrombin level when marstacimab was administered alone (82 to 172nM,
88 to 166nM, and 174nM) or in combination with rFVIIa (102 to 178nM, 99 to 57nM, and
167nM) was comparable in plasma of patients HA with or without inhibitors and HB without
inhibitor, respectively, demonstrating minimal additive effect.
Table 78. Peak Thrombin Levels for Marstacimab and rFVIIa When Used Alone or in Combination

Source: Table 10.1, page 14 of Study 122443 report
Vehicle=50% phosphate buffered saline/50% HEPES buffer; PF-06741086=marstacimab.
Abbreviations: n, number of participants in specific group; rFVIIa, recombinant factor VIIa

4 Inhibitor: neutralizing antibody to the injected factors, FVIII or FIX
5 Bypass agent: a treatment that helps blood clot by activating the coagulation cascade without the need for FVIII or

FIX

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Marstacimab When Used in Combination With aPCC, Study #045123
The effect of marstacimab in promoting coagulation when used in combination with plasma
derived aPCC was evaluated using the TGA in platelet poor plasma from patients with HA with
inhibitor and patients with HB with inhibitor. The peak thrombin level using the TGA is shown
in Table 79. The peak thrombin levels when marstacimab was used in combination with aPCC
(209.3 to 240.6nM and 254.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) were higher than
marstacimab (72.9 to 109.7nM and 70.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) or aPCC (74.7
to 110.1nM and 163.1nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) alone. However, the thrombin
levels when used in combination were still within the range of marstacimab in nonhemophilic
plasma (182.1 to 256.7nM and 160.4nM in hemophilia A and B, respectively) which would have
full complement of coagulation factors.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Study B7841001
A randomized, double-blind, sponsor-open, placebo-controlled, single IV or SC dose escalation
study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of PF-06741086 (marstacimab) in healthy
participants and an open-label evaluation in healthy Japanese participants.
Primary Objective
To determine the safety and tolerability of marstacimab administered to healthy participants as
single escalating SC administered doses or single escalating IV infused doses.
Study Design
This was a first-in-human, randomized, double-blind, sponsor-open, placebo-controlled study to
investigate the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of SC and IV administration of marstacimab. The
study was conducted in seven planned cohorts of healthy male adult participants (Cohorts 1
through 7) and in an additional cohort of healthy Japanese male participants (Cohort 8).
Participants were enrolled in a dose escalating fashion and randomized to either marstacimab or
placebo (3:1 ratio) at doses of 30 mg (N=4), 100 mg (N=8), and 300 mg (N=8) subcutaneously
125
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
and 150 mg (N=8), 300 mg (N=8), and 440 mg (N=8) intravenously. Japanese participants were
enrolled into an additional single dose cohort (N=4) at marstacimab 300 mg SC. The
pharmacokinetic sampling were done at predose, and 1, 4, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours, and 7, 14, 21, 28,
35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days after SC dosing and at predose, and 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours,
and 7, 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days after IV dosing. The pharmacodynamic samples
such as TFPI level, TGA, prothrombin fragment 1+2, D-dimer, and diluted prothrombin time
were collected at the same time as PK samples. The immunogenicity samples were collected at
predose and 14, 21, 28, 35, 42, 56, 70, and 84 days postdose.
Results
Summary of PK parameters for marstacimab following single dose administration are provided
in Table 80. The median plasma marstacimab concentration versus time profiles for SC and IV
administration are provided in Figure 19. The PK parameters were not determined for the 30 mg
SC cohort due to all concentrations below limit of quantification (BLQ). The Applicant noted
that participants in the 150 mg IV cohort were slightly under-dosed due to an incorrect pump
setting; however, the actual dose of 147 mg was accurately captured. The median time to reach
Cmax was 48 to 108 hours following SC administration. Regardless of route of administration
(SC or IV), the exposure of marstacimab (Cmax and AUC) increased with increasing dose in a
greater than dose proportional manner. The clearance also decreased with increasing dose and
the half-life increased from 33.3 hours to 65.8 hrs following 100 mg and 300 mg SC
administration, respectively. The nonlinear PK is likely due to the target-mediated drug
disposition. The exposure was comparable between Japanese and non-Japanese participants
following 300 mg SC administration.

Study B7841002
A multicenter, open-label, multiple ascending dose study to evaluate the safety, tolerability,
pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, and efficacy of subcutaneous or intravenous PF06741086 in participants with severe hemophilia.
Primary Objective
To determine the safety and tolerability of multiple doses of PF-06741086 administered to severe
hemophilia A and B participants with and without inhibitors.
Study Design
This was a Phase 1b/2, open-label, multiple ascending dose study in male participants with
severe hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitors. The study consisted of four cohorts,
starting at marstacimab 300 mg SC once weekly (Cohort 1, N=8, noninhibitors). Based on the
safety, tolerability, and PK data through Day 29, lower dose of 150 mg SC once weekly with a
loading dose of 300 mg (Cohort 2, N=6, noninhibitors) and higher dose of 450 mg SC once
weekly (Cohort 3, N=6, noninhibitors) were initiated. Cohort 4 was originally intended for
studying marstacimab 1000 mg IV once a month dosing regimen, however, Cohort 4 was
modified to receive marstacimab 300 mg SC once weekly for participants with inhibitors only
(N=7). The participants received treatment up to 3 months. The pharmacokinetic and
pharmacodynamic sampling was done on Days 1, 2, 4, 8, 15, 22, 29, 30, 33, 57, 85, and 113. The
immunogenicity sampling was done on Days 1, 15, 29, 57, 85 and 113.

133
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Results
Summary of PK parameters following weekly marstacimab doses are provided in Table 83. The
median plasma marstacimab concentration versus time profiles for marstacimab are provided in
Figure 25. After multiple doses on Day 29 (Week 5), exposure (AUCtau and Cmax) increased
greater than dose-proportional over the range of 150 mg to 450 mg. Based on the trough
concentrations, steady state appeared to have been achieved by Day 57. There was no significant
difference in the exposure between participants with or without inhibitors based on marstacimab
300 mg weekly dose.

Study B7841009
A Phase 1, open-label, randomized, 4-period, 2-sequence, crossover study to evaluate the
bioequivalence of marstacimab (PF-06741086) PFS device and PFP device following
subcutaneous administration in healthy adult male participants.

136
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Primary Objective
To compare the single dose PK of marstacimab (300 mg) administered subcutaneously with a
PFP device to that with the PFS device in healthy adult male participants.
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, open-label, randomized, single dose, 4-period, 2-sequence, fully replicate
crossover study in healthy adult male participants. A single dose of 300 mg marstacimab (given
as two single injections of 150 mg) was administered subcutaneously into the abdomen on Day 1
of each period, following a standardized breakfast. Dose administration in each of the four period
was alternated between the top half and bottom half of the abdomen. The pharmacokinetic
sampling was conducted at predose, and 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours, and 7, 14, 21 days after
dosing. The immunogenicity samples were collected at predose (on Day 1 prior to dosing) and
on Day 21.
Results
The study was terminated early due to a serious adverse event (SAE) of pulmonary embolism in
one participant. A total of 18 participants completed at least 1 period and contributed to data for
calculation of PK parameters. Summary of mean plasma PK parameters and statistical
comparison of PFS and PFP are provided in Table 84 and Table 85. The plasma concentration
versus time curve is shown in Figure 27. The ratios (90% CI) of geometric means of Cmax and
AUClast were 104.1% (93.7 to 115.6%) and 107.5% (95.2 to 121.4%) showing comparable
exposure when administered via PFP and PFS. The median time to reach maximum
concentration was 72 hours for both PFP and PFS with overlapping range of 60 to 146 hours. As
the sample size was smaller than planned, a sensitivity analysis was conducted using data from
participants who completed at least 2 periods (N=15). The results showed that the geometric
mean ratios of Cmax and AUClast were still comparable (Cmax: 107.7% [98.8 to 117.5%] and AUC:
111.9% [101.1 to 123.9%]). A total of six participants tested positive for ADAs and of those, two
participants positive for NAb.

Study B7841010
A Phase 1, single-arm, open-label, nonrandomized, noncontrolled multicenter study to evaluate
the pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, and tolerability of a single subcutaneous dose
of PF-06741086 in Chinese adult participants with severe hemophilia.
Primary Objective
To determine the safety and tolerability of a single SC dose of marstacimab administered to
Chinese adult participants with severe hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors.
Study Design
This study was a single-arm, open-label, nonrandomized, noncontrolled, multicenter study in
Chinese adult participants between ages 18 to <75 years with severe hemophilia A or B, with or
without inhibitor. All participants (N=6) received a single subcutaneous injection of marstacimab
300 mg (2 x 150 mg) on Day 1. The pharmacokinetic and PD blood samples were collected at
predose and 1, 4, 12, 24, 48, 72 hours and 14, 21, 28 days postdose. The immunogenicity
samples were collected at predose and Days 14, 21, and 28 of the study.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
14.3.1. Bioanalytical Method for PK Assessment
Plasma marstacimab concentrations were assayed using a validated electrochemiluminescence
(ECL) method. Standards, controls, and test samples were incubated with TFPI (1.0 μg/mL),
which has been immobilized on a 96-well MSD standard plate. After incubation, unbound
material is washed away and marstacimab bound to the plate is incubated with a primary
detection antibody, Mu anti-TFPI Ab. Following incubation, the plate is washed and bound
marstacimab is incubated with a secondary detection antibody, goat anti-mouse IgG Sulfo TAG
(1.0 μg/mL). After a final wash, 2X read buffer containing tripropylamine is added immediately
prior to reading the plate on an MSD Sector Imager 600 plate reader generating a
chemiluminescent signal. The signal is directly proportional to the amount of marstacimab in the
sample.
An overview of marstacimab ECL method validation parameters and assay performance are
provided in Table 87 and Table 88. For all clinical studies, 2 ECL methods were developed and
(b) (4)
validated (i.e., method
15-1536 for Studies B7841001, B7841002, B7841003, B7841005,
(b) (4)
B7841007 and B7841009 and method
P7615MVHuP1AP for Study B7841005 and

HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

14.3.2. Bioanalytical Method for PD Assessment
For PD characterization, plasma samples were analyzed for total TFPI, TGA (comprised of lag
time, peak thrombin and endogenous thrombin generation potential), d-dimer, prothrombin
fragment (PF) 1+2, dPT. All PD parameters were measured using laboratory developed test
validated under CAP/CLIA requirements except for total TFPI, which was analyzed using
validated bioanalytical method. Total TFPI, which includes marstacimab bound and unbound
forms, was extracted from human plasma using a biotinylated noncompeting anti-TFPI antibody.
This TFPI antibody complex was captured using streptavidin magnetic beads. Because TFPI is
an endogenous component in human plasma, the method for TFPI quantitation was validated in
mouse plasma as surrogate matrix.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
14.4.1. Summary of Clinical Studies
Immunogenicity of marstacimab was evaluated in three Phase 1 studies (Studies B7841009,
B7841010, B7841001), two Phase 1/2 studies (Studies B7841002 and B7841003), and three
Phase 3 studies (Studies B7841005, B7841007, B7841008). Among the Phase 3 studies, only
Study B7841005 has available immunogenicity information, which is specifically from one of
two subgroups, the noninhibitor subgroup because the study of inhibitor subgroup is still
ongoing. Therefore, information in the draft label is coming from the noninhibitor cohort of the
Phase 3 Study B7841005. The submitted clinical study results included participants who
received 12-month treatment. Table 89 contains the assessment of immunogenicity information
up to 12 months for Study B7841005. Overall study design in terms of treatment duration and
sampling time and size was adequate to assess the ADA impact on PK.

14.4.2. Highlight of Key Characteristics of
Immunogenicity Assays Relevant to This Review
Table 90 and Table 91 show several assay characteristics of the ADA and NAb assay
respectively, that are relevant for the analysis.
The ADA assay can detect 100 ng/mL of positive control in the presence of 25 μg/mL of
marstacimab. A fraction (16%) of the ADA samples had drug concentration greater than the
ADA drug tolerance (25 μg/mL). Therefore, the presence of marstacimab may interfere with
detection of ADA in 16% of study samples.
The NAb assay can detect 1200 ng/mL of positive control in the presence of 50 μg/mL of
marstacimab. A fraction (2.6%) of the NAb samples had drug concentration greater than the
NAb drug tolerance of 50 mg/mL. Therefore, the presence of marstacimab may interfere with
detection of NAb in 2.6% of study samples.

14.4.3. Methods for Evaluating the Effect of
Immunogenicity on PK of Marstacimab
To evaluate the impact of immunogenicity on PK, the observed concentrations in two groups
(ADA+ and ADA-) was compared with time-matched PK and ADA data using Immunogenicity
Specimen (IS) tool developed in-house. At each timepoint, marstacimab geometric mean
concentrations were tabulated by ADA+ and ADA- groups. For the statistical comparisons of
concentration data between ADA+ and ADA- groups, the geometric mean ratio (GMR) of
ADA+/ADA- and the corresponding 90% CI are presented.

146
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
This review does not include the evaluation of NAb effect on PK.

14.4.4. Effect of Immunogenicity on PK of
Marstacimab – Results
The results of the IS analysis in Study B7841005, specifically, the effect of ADA on PK is
presented. Table 92 summarizes the marstacimab geometric mean concentrations in ADA+ and
ADA- participants during the treatment period. The analysis of marstacimab concentration from
ADA+ and ADA- samples during the treatment period and 90% confidence interval of GMR of
drug concentration at each visit is provided in Figure 29. Marstacimab concentration is lower in
ADA+ participants. The IS analysis results show that the GMR (ADA+/ADA-) ranged from 0.50
to 0.76 through the study duration of 360 days. In addition, the 90% CI of the GMR is entirely
below 1.0 at 6 of 8 time points over the study duration (i.e., except for Days 180 and 300).
Therefore, development of ADA decreases the concentration of marstacimab.

14.4.5. Effect of Immunogenicity on Efficacy of
Marstacimab
The impact of immunogenicity on efficacy of marstacimab was evaluated based on comparison
of the clinical outcome (i.e., annualized bleeding rate [ABR]) in ADA+ and ADA- participants.
The summary of the ABR of treated bleeds (primary endpoint) by ADA status is provided in
Table 93. Based on the results, the ADA impact on marstacimab PK did not affect clinical
outcome. The mean ABR was 3.34 for ADA+ participants whereas the mean ABR was 4.92 in
ADA- participants.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Summary of Applicant’s Population PK and
Pharmacodynamic Modeling Analyses
Both population pharmacokinetic (PPK) and pharmacodynamic (PKPD) modeling analyses were
conducted to support the dose selection for adult and adolescent participants with hemophilia.
The objectives of Applicant’s modeling analysis were:
•

To develop an integrated, predictive PPK model for marstacimab plasma drug and TFPI
concentrations.

•

To identify potential covariates which impact marstacimab concentration levels.

•

To explore the relationship between free target (TFPI) and peak thrombin.

Method
NONMEM version 7.5.0 was used for all model estimations. Perl-speaks-NONMEM (PsN)
version 5.3.0 was used for visual predictive check (VPC) and/or bootstrap simulations; R 4.1.3
was used for pre- and post-processing and for summarizing and plotting results. R package
mrgsolve (version 1.0.4) was used for generating simulated PK and PKPD results. Marstacimab
PK was described using a TMDD model define in terms of clearance (CL), Vc, Q, Vp, first-order
absorption rate constant (ka), and absorption lag time (Tlag). Both drug concentration data and
total receptor (TFPI) data were used to estimate model parameters. Separate residual error
models were included in the model for PK and total TFPI. A PKPD model was developed to
describe peak thrombin and its relationship to free TFPI predicted from the final PPK model
using an Emax type of model. At all stages of model development, goodness-of-fit plots were
examined to assess model adequacy, possible lack of fit, or violation of assumptions.
Results
The PPK model was developed based on all Phase 1 and 2 studies, and the Phase 3 Study
B7841005 in participants with hemophilia A and B. The proposed dosing regimen (applied in
Phase 3) is 300 mg marstacimab SC for a loading dose followed by 150 mg SC weekly (QW)
maintenance dose. Meanwhile, individual participants who met protocol specified dose
escalation criteria based upon breakthrough bleed were allowed to have the dose increased to
300 mg SC QW.
The PPK analysis was conducted using 2235 evaluable marstacimab concentrations in a total of
150 participants and 63 healthy participants. The summary of studies used for PPK analysis are
listed in Table 94. Covariate analysis identified the covariates in Table 95 for the parameter
estimates in the final model. In summary, the effect of population type (healthy versus
hemophilia participants), ADA participant status (ADA positive versus ADA negative), hepatic
function, race category (Asians versus non-Asians), renal impairment (normal versus mild renal
impairment) and hemophilia type (hemophilia A versus hemophilia B) were estimated. Their

150

Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369

effect on pharmacokinetics of marstacimab was not considered significant after accounting for
the weight-adjusted effect on CL.

The PK of marstacimab and TFPI were best characterized by a semi-mechanistic, twocompartment TMDD model with quasi-steady state approximation and additional nonlinear
clearance of the drug in the model. Normalized weight was included as a structural covariate in
the model. The final marstacimab PK model parameter estimates, and the corresponding 95%
confidence intervals (CIs) are presented in Table 96.

concentration at steady state (Cmin,ss), maximum concentration at steady state (Cmax,ss), average
concentration at steady state (Cavg,ss), accumulation ratio, effective half-life (t1/2) of marstacimab.
A summary of these parameters is provided in Table 97. The secondary parameters were
calculated for all hemophilia participants studied in the program using the empirical Bayesian
estimates of the model parameters from the final population PK model and simulating a dosing
regimen of either 150 mg or 300 mg weekly dosing.

The review team found the Applicant’s TMDD PPK model adequate to describe the marstacimab
serum concentration-time profiles in the observed range following the administration of
marstacimab in participants with hemophilia A and B from all clinical studies. The residual plots
didn’t indicate any obvious bias in the parameter estimates. Therefore, the PK model is
acceptable for simulating post-hoc exposure metrics, e.g., Cavg,ss, Cmax,ss, Cmin,ss, and AUCss of
marstacimab for exposure-response (E-R) analyses of efficacy or safety measurements.
Covariate analysis identified body weight as a significant covariate and applied to the clearance
and volume distribution in the final PPK model. In addition, other covariates such as the effect of
population type (healthy versus hemophilia patients), ADA participant status (ADA positive
versus ADA negative), hepatic function, race category (Asians versus Non-Asians), renal
impairment (normal versus mild renal impairment) and hemophilia type (hemophilia A versus
hemophilia B) were not identified as significant covariates for the marstacimab exposure after
accounting for the weight-adjusted effect on CL.

efficacy endpoint of ABR to better understand the appropriateness of the proposed dosing
regimen of marstacimab.

14.5.2. Exposure-Response PK Analysis
The Applicant conducted the E-R analysis of efficacy using marstacimab PK and the primary
efficacy endpoint, annualized bleed rate (ABR). The objective of the Applicant’s E-R analysis
was to support the dose justification for adult and adolescent participants with hemophilia.
Method
A Poisson regression analysis was used to describe the relationship between marstacimab
exposure metrics (steady state Cavg, Cmax and Cmin) and number of bleeds for hemophilia
participants. Doses evaluated were 150 mg SC QW (loading dose of 300 mg SC), 300 mg SC
QW and 450 mg SC QW. The recommended clinical dose is 150 mg SC QW (loading dose of
300 mg SC), with a provision to escalate the dose to 300 mg SC QW. The marstacimab Cavg,ss
was calculated using post-hoc estimates obtained from the final PPK model. Age was explored in
the model as a continuous covariate (not as separate age groups) and showed only a small effect
on ABR.
Results
The E-R analysis included number of bleeds for hemophilia patients in Phase 2 (Studies
B7841002 and B7841003) and Phase 3 (Study B7841005) studies. The final model was a QuasiPoisson regression model with log PK as the independent variable and ABR as the dependent
161
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
variable, which did not include any covariates. Figure 40 shows the model fits of the final QuasiPoisson model for marstacimab Cavg,ss vs. ABR, which indicates a negative relationship between
examined marstacimab exposure and ABR.
Figure 40. Model Fits for the Final E-R Model of ABR With Log of Cavg at Steady-State

Source: Response to FDA Information Request-Received February 29, 2024 – Question 1, Figure 1
The x-axis shows steady state exposure metric in ng/mL of Cavg,ss. The y-axis shows ABR values (predicted from the Quasi-Poisson

respectively for each bin of exposure metric in the analysis population. The blue line, (and blue shaded area) are the
model-predicted mean (and 95% CI) ABR for the range of observed exposure metric in the analysis population. The numbers above
the error bars show the observed mean ABR values for each bin of exposure metric.
Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rates, Cavg, average plasma concentration; Cavg,ss, average plasma concentration at
steady-state, CI, confidence interval; E-R, exposure-response

To evaluate whether the proposed dose is reasonable, E-R analysis was conducted with primary
efficacy endpoint of ABR. The LOESS nonparametric regression method with data from the
Phase 1b/2 study (Study B7841002), Phase 2 open-label extension study (Study B7841003), and
Phase 3 study (Study B7841005) for doses 150, 300, and 450 mg was used in the E-R analysis.
Fourteen participants who had dose escalated during the Phase 3 study were not included due to
limited PK samples after dose escalation. Figure 41 shows that there is a slight trend for decrease
in ABR with increase in marstacimab exposure (Cavg,ss).

The review team found a similar trend as the Applicant’s model, results of the E-R analysis show
a trend for decreasing ABR with increasing concentrations, which is also consistent with the
results from the previous PKPD analysis.
14.5.2.1. Phase 3 Dose Justification for Adolescents
The justification for same dose (150 mg SC once weekly, with 300 mg SC loading dose) for
adolescents and adults in the Phase 3 study (Study B7841005) was based on the predictions from
the previous PPK and PKPD model. To understand the weight effect on PK exposure metrics
(steady state Cavg, Cmax and Cmin), the Applicant simulated a population with a uniform weight
distribution in the range of 30 to 120 kg. 5000 participants were simulated in total, with
approximately 500 participants in each weight category (every 10 kg increment). The
pharmacokinetic exposure metrics were predicted from the final PPK model.
Box plots of simulated steady-state average for 150 mg SC QW across weight categories with
observed data overlaid are shown in Figure 42. PK simulations performed for weight categories
based on observed weight distribution show that exposures (median Cavg,ss) were predicted to be
around 4-fold higher for the 30 to 50 kg group and about 6-fold lower for the 100 to 130 kg
group in comparison to predicted exposures for a reference (standard) weight group of 70 to
80 kg. In general, at lower weights, the higher concentrations were still below the maximum
concentrations seen in clinical studies. At higher weights, concentrations were above the
marstacimab plasma EC50 based on modeling of Phase 1 and 2 clinical data. In summary, an
overlap was seen for the observed range of concentrations across the 40 to 50 kg to 80 to 90 kg
weight categories.

163
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection

The review team found the Applicant’s exposure response model to be acceptable. A slight trend
for decreasing ABR with increasing concentrations was found by the E-R analyses. The 150 mg
SC QW weekly with 300 mg SC loading dose is acceptable for most patients with a dose
escalation that may benefit a small proportion of patients whose ABR is not adequately
controlled. Overall, E-R and PKPD analyses support the proposed dosing regimen of
marstacimab for the proposed indication, which is based upon the favorable safety and efficacy
results of the studied clinical studies.

Based on Amendment 7, March 24, 2022)

Phase
3
Source: FDA table based on Clinical Protocol

Rationale
Treatment with marstacimab is anticipated to demonstrate a clinically relevant advantage or a
major contribution to patient care in comparison to current methods of treatment for hemophilia
A or B because it works differently than factor replacement products and its anticipated efficacy
is not expected to be impacted by the presence of inhibitors. The potential for once weekly (QW)
subcutaneous (SC) administration provides for treatment options in the absence of reliable
vascular access, increased convenience and may enable better compliance. Combined, these
qualities should result in a reduction of bleeding episodes.
Objectives
Primary Objectives
•

To demonstrate the efficacy and safety of marstacimab for routine prophylaxis in severe
hemophilia A or moderately severe to severe hemophilia B (FVIII activity <1% or FIX
activity ≤2%, respectively) participants 12 to <75 years of age with or without inhibitors

Secondary Objectives
•

To evaluate additional efficacy of marstacimab

•

To evaluate the effect of marstacimab on Health-related quality of life (HRQoL)

165
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Tertiary/Exploratory Objectives
•
•
•
•

Assess PK and PD parameters in this study population
To evaluate the effect of marstacimab on Health-related quality of life
To compare joint health post marstacimab to baseline
To evaluate the effect of marstacimab on total coagulation factor or bypass product
consumption

Overall Design
This is a one-way, cross-over, open-label, multi-center study in approximately 145 adolescent
and adult participants between ages 12 to <75 years with severe hemophilia A or moderately
severe to severe hemophilia B (defined as FVIII activity <1% or FIX activity ≤2%, respectively)
with or without inhibitor, with approximately 20% of participants as adolescents (ages between
12 to <18 years old). Additional participants (approximately 20%) may be recruited for this
study in order to account for attrition of enrolled participants and to provide for sufficient
representation into regions which have experienced recruitment delays due to the COVID-19
pandemic. This study is comparing treatment with their prescribed factor replacement therapy or
bypass therapy during an observational phase with a 12-month Active Treatment Phase, during
which participants will receive prophylaxis (defined as treatment by SC injection of marstacimab
[PF-06741086]).
The dosing regimen is marstacimab 300 mg SC for initial loading dose followed by 150 mg SC
QW. Individual participants who meet protocol specified dose escalation criteria based upon
breakthrough bleeding, may have the dose increased to 300 mg SC QW.
Disclosure Statement
This is a Cross-Over Prevention study with one arm which has no masking
Number of Participants
•
•
•
•

Approximately 145 participants (approximately 20% adolescents) will be enrolled:
Approximately 45 participants (approximately 35 hemophilia A and 10 hemophilia B
participants) in the Inhibitor Cohort;
Approximately 100 participants (approximately 80 hemophilia A and 20 hemophilia B
participants) in the Non-Inhibitor Cohort.
The Inhibitor Cohort (n=45) will include at least 5 adolescent participants and the NonInhibitor Cohort will include at least 20 adolescent participants.

Intervention Groups and Duration
Enrollment of individuals into the Observational Phase who are on prior on-demand or
prophylaxis treatment will begin at the same time.

166
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Prior On-Demand Treatment
Participants with either hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitors, and who are prescribed an
on-demand treatment regimen during the observational phase will transition to the Active
Treatment Phase after approximately 6 months. Individuals with inhibitors will receive ondemand treatment with recombinant factor VIIa (rFVIIa), Prothrombin Complex Concentrates
(PCC), or activated Prothrombin Complex Concentrates (aPCC) agents e.g., FEIBA or in regions
where available, BYCLOT (Inhibitor Cohort) and individuals without inhibitors will receive on
on-demand treatment with FVIII- or FIX-replacement therapy (noninhibitor cohort) during the
observational phase before crossing over to the 12-month active treatment phase.
Prior Prophylaxis Treatment
Participants without inhibitors who were on prior prophylactic treatment with FVIII- or FIXreplacement (defined as treatment by intravenous injection of factor concentrate to prevent
bleeding) during the observational phase will transition to the Active Treatment Phase after
approximately 6 months.
Study Duration
The anticipated study duration for an individual participant is approximately 21 months,
including an approximately 45-day Screening period, an observational phase of approximately 6
months, a 12-month active treatment phase during which the participant receives an initial
loading dose followed by prophylaxis treatment with PF-06741086, and a 1-month follow-up for
safety monitoring.

Study Population
Inclusion Criteria
1. Male age 12 to <75 years inclusive with a minimum body weight of 35 kg.
2. Participants with a diagnosis of severe hemophilia A or moderately severe to severe
hemophilia B (FVIII activity <1% or FIX activity ≤2%, respectively) documented by
clinical laboratory prior to enrollment. The severity of hemophilia may be confirmed
either by documented historical evidence from a clinical laboratory prior to Screening or
by factor activity obtained from a clinical laboratory (which may include the central
laboratory for this study) prior to enrollment.
3. Participants who are enrolled into the non-inhibitor cohort must also meet the following
criteria:

168
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
•

No detectable or documented history of inhibitors (≥0.6 BU/mL or greater than the upper
limit of normal [ULN] for the testing laboratory) against FVIII or FIX prior to enrollment
(baseline of observational phase).

•

Subjects receiving routine prophylaxis treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement, have
demonstrated at least 80% compliance with scheduled prophylaxis regimen during 6
months prior to enrollment, and willing to continue to receive routine prophylaxis
treatment with FVIII/FIX replacement during the observational phase.
OR

•

Subject with on-demand treatment regimen with ≥6 acute bleeding episodes (spontaneous
and/or traumatic) that required coagulation factor infusion during the 6 months period
prior to screening and willing to continue to receive on-demand treatment during the
observational phase. Surgical bleeding episodes do not apply to this criterion.

Exclusion Criteria
1. Previous or current treatment for or history of coronary artery diseases, venous or arterial
thrombosis (Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events [CTCAE] Grade >1), or
ischemic disease (except for catheter-associated thrombosis).
2. Known planned surgical procedure during the planned study period.
3. Known hemostatic defect other than hemophilia A or B.
4. Abnormal renal or hepatic function as defined by the following laboratory results at
screening:
a. Alanine transaminase (ALT) >2 × ULN.
b. Bilirubin >1.5 × ULN (isolated bilirubin >1.5 × ULN is acceptable if bilirubin is
fractionated and direct bilirubin <35%).
c. Current unstable liver or biliary disease per investigator assessment defined by the
presence of ascites, encephalopathy, coagulopathy, hypoalbuminaemia,
esophageal or gastric varices, persistent jaundice, or cirrhosis. NOTE: Stable
chronic liver disease (including Gilbert’s syndrome, asymptomatic gallstones, and
chronic stable hepatitis B or C -eg, presence of hepatitis B surface antigen
[HBsAg] or positive hepatitis C antibody test result at screening or within 3
months prior to starting study intervention) is acceptable if the participant
otherwise meets entry criteria.
a. Serum albumin less than the lower limit of normal (LLN).
b. Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) <30 mL/min/1.73 m2.
5. Abnormal hematology values as defined by the following laboratory tests at Screening:
a. Platelet count <100,000/uL
b. Hemoglobin level <10 g/dL
6. Other acute or chronic medical or psychiatric condition including recent (within the past
year) or active suicidal ideation or behavior or laboratory abnormality that may increase
the risk associated with study participation or investigational product administration or
may interfere with the interpretation of study results and, in the judgment of the
investigator, would make the participant inappropriate for entry into this study.

169
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
7. QTcF >450 msec or QTcF >480 msec in subjects with bundle branch block.
8. Participants with hypersensitivity or an allergic reaction to hamster protein or other
components of the study intervention.
9. Current routine prophylaxis with bypassing agent (e.g., aPCC, BYCLOT, Prothrombin
Complex Concentrates [PCC], or rFVIIa), noncoagulation nonfactor replacement therapy
(e.g., emicizumab), or any previous treatment with a gene therapy product for treatment
of hemophilia.
• Participants with inhibitors who are being treated using a prophylaxis treatment
regimen with a bypass agent will be considered on a cae-be-case basis, only after
discussion and agreement between the investigator and the Pfizer medical monitor.
• Participants who have previously received nonfactor-based hemophilia therapy (e.g.,
fitusiran, concizumab, emicizumab) will be considered on a case-by-case basis, only
after discussion and agreement between the investigator and the Pfizer medical
monitor.
10. Regular, concomitant therapy with immunomodulatory drugs (e.g., IV immunoglobulin
[IVIG], and routine systemic corticosteroids, rituximab).
11. Ongoing or planned use of immune tolerance induction during the OP or ATP, or
prophylaxis with FVIII or FIX replacement at any time after initiation of treatment with
study intervention during the ATP.
12. Participation in other studies involving investigational drug(s) or investigational
vaccine(s) within 30 days (or as determined by local requirements) or 5 half-lives prior to
study entry or during study participation.
13. Previous exposure to marstacimab during to participation in Studies B7841002 and
B7841003.
14. CD4 cell count ≤200/uL if human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)-positive.
15. Screening 12-lead ECG that demonstrates clinically relevant abnormalities that may
affect participant safety or interpretation of study results.
Treatment of Breakthrough Bleeding
During OP: Patients experience acute bleeding episode should be stabilized utilizing the patient’s
standard hemostatic treatment regimen, which may include FVIII- or FIX replacement therapy.
During the ATP: Patients experience breakthrough bleeding judge by the investigator or treating
physician to require additional hemostatic therapy beyond PF-06741086 prophylaxis, FVIII- or
FIX- or FIX-replacement products may be administered at the lowest effective dose and
according to the approved product labeling.
Prohibited Medications
Immunomodulatory medications (e.g., IVIG, routine systemic corticosteroids, rituximab),
emicizumab, bypassing agent (rFVIIa, PCC, aPCC, or BYCLOT) were prohibited throughout all
phases of the study.
Systemic antifibrinolytic agents or medications known to influence platelet function (e.g., aspirin
or certain non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) require a washout of ≥120 hours prior to

170
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
administration of study intervention on Day 0 through completion of ATP Day 360 (Visit 20) in
the Active Treatment Phase.
Study Intervention Compliance
Participant compliance with completion of eDiary and study intervention injections will be
assessed at each visit. Compliance will be assessed by review of the eDiary. Participants must
maintain at least 80% compliance with weekly dosing of PF-06741086.
Dose Modification
The dose may be escalated after 6 months of active treatment with marstacimab, and at any time
after completion of Visit 14 (ATP Day 180), from 150 mg SC QW to 300 mg SC QW if the
following criterion is met:
•

Weigh at least 50 kg and not adolescent.

•

Two or more spontaneous (atraumatic) bleeds (consisting of joint bleeds or significant soft
tissue/muscle or other site bleeds) treated with infusion(s) of coagulation FVIII or FIX over a
6-month period in the absence of a confirmed FVIII or FIX inhibitor, respectively.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Derivation of the Noninferiority Margins for
Major Efficacy Endpoints for Routine
Prophylaxis
The section described the Applicant’s derivation of the noninferiority (NI) margins for the
bleeding-related primary and key secondary endpoints for routine prophylaxis.
Noninferiority Margin for the Primary Endpoint (ABR for Treated Bleeds)
In the statistical analysis plan (SAP) v6 dated January 31, 2023, the Applicant stated that
historical data were selected from among the Applicant’s hemophilia studies. This enabled
selection of individual participant level data that would closely match the inclusion/exclusion
criteria of Study B7841005. In selecting studies, the following aspects were considered: (1)
having both prophylaxis and on-demand treatment, providing a within-study treatment difference
estimate, (2) having an overlapping age group of 12 to 74 years old to match Study B7841005

185
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
age group, and (3) not being a regional study. The following three studies satisfied these criteria:
B1821010 (BeneFIX), B1821002 (BeneFIX), and B1831004 (Xyntha/ReFacto).
•

B1821010 was an open-label 1-way crossover study comparing the efficacy and safety
between on-demand treatment and prophylactic therapy using BeneFIX in hemophilia B
participants age 12 to 65 years old and factor IX activity level not greater than 2%. This
study comprised Period 1 (on-demand treatment at a dosage determined by principal
investigator, 6 months) and Period 2 (100 international units [IU]/kg once-weekly dose, 12
months).

•

B1821002 was an open-label, randomized, 4-period crossover study in males with
hemophilia B, age 6 to 65 years old with factor IX activity level not greater than 2% intended
to compare on-demand administration and two prophylaxis regimens. The study was
comprised the following four periods:
­ Period 1: On-demand administration per dose directed by investigators, 16 weeks.
­ Period 2: 50 IU/kg/ twice weekly or 100 IU/kg/once weekly (randomized), 16 weeks.
­ Period 3: On-demand administration per dose directed by investigators, 8 weeks.
­ Period 4: 50 IU/kg/twice weekly or 100 IU/kg/once weekly (different regimen than that
applied during Period 2), 16 weeks.

•

B1831004 evaluated the safety and efficacy of ReFacto AF in hemophilia A participants
switching to ReFacto AF from ReFacto or other factor VIII products in usual care settings.
The participants were age 12 and greater and had factor XIII activity level <1%. All
participants were treated under standard care with ReFacto AF at a dose and frequency
prescribed by the treating physician. The duration of treatment depended on the frequency of
infusions (to achieve 100 exposure days) with mean (SD) of 336 (223) days.

Table 100 presents the Applicant’s analyses of the ABR of treated bleeds based on participantlevel data from three hemophilia studies with inclusion/exclusion criteria that closely matched
those for Study B7841005. The mean ABR difference between prophylaxis and on-demand
treatment was estimated using three analyses: t-test allowing for nonhomogeneous variances,
Hodges-Lehman location-shift parameter estimation, and additive negative binomial model. All
analyses resulted in similar estimated mean differences of approximately 21, and the lower
bound of 2-sided 95% confidence intervals ranging from 16.8 to 17.6.
During the review, the review team sent an IR and requested the Applicant to (1) clarify if the
three studies [i.e., B1821010 (BeneFIX), B1821002 (BeneFIX), and B1831004
(Xyntha/ReFacto)] were based on hemophilia A or B participants with and without inhibitors, or
based only on participants without inhibitors; and (2) provide the potential NI margins for all
major efficacy endpoints based only on participants without inhibitors. The Applicant responded
that the three internal studies included only enrolled participants without inhibitors.

Based on the lower bound of the confidence interval of this estimate, the ABR was assumed to
be higher in patients receiving on-demand compared to prophylaxis by at least 16.8, which was
considered margin 1 (M1) in the noninferiority test setting.
Given the large effect size of prophylaxis treatment, an appropriate value for margin 2 (M2) was
considered in order to preserve a sufficiently large proportion of this effect. A value of 2.5 for
M2 (~85% effect preserved) was selected as both clinically meaningful and yielding a reasonable
sample size.
Noninferiority Margin for Key Secondary Endpoint (ABR for Joint Bleeds)
The Applicant conducted analyses based on the two BeneFIX studies since the ReFacto study did
not utilize this endpoint. All analyses resulted in similar estimated mean ABR differences of
approximately 21 with the lower bound of 2-sided 95% confidence intervals ranging from 15.2
to 16.5. Therefore, the on-demand ABR was assumed to be higher than the prophylaxis ABR by
at least 15.2, which was considered M1 in the noninferiority test setting. Given the large effect
size of prophylaxis treatment, an appropriate value for M2 was considered in order to preserve a
sufficiently large proportion of this effect. A value of 2.5 for M2 (~84% effect preserved) was
selected as clinically meaningful for a planned sample size of 60.
Noninferiority Margin for Key Secondary Endpoint (ABR for Spontaneous Bleeds)
The Applicant conducted analyses based on the two BeneFIX studies since the ReFacto study did
not utilize this endpoint. Results from the t-test and negative binomial regression were similar
with the estimated ABR mean difference of 18.6 and similar 95% CIs. The result from Hodges-

187
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
Lehman estimation was larger with 21.8 (16.0, 27.4) for the estimated ABR median difference
and its 95% CI.
All analyses indicated that the on-demand ABR was higher than the prophylaxis ABR by at least
13.2, which was considered M1 in the noninferiority test setting. Given the large effect size of
prophylaxis treatment, an appropriate value for M2 was considered in order to preserve a
sufficiently large proportion of this effect. A value of 2.5 for M2 (~81% effect preserved) was
selected as clinically meaningful for a planned sample size of 60.
Noninferiority Margin for Key Secondary Endpoint (ABR for Target Joints Bleeds)
The incidence of target joint bleeds was not an efficacy endpoint in any of the three hemophilia
studies in which the estimates for several bleed count endpoints were derived. In the absence of
internal data, the Applicant relied on the results of emicizumab publications of HAVEN1 and
HAVEN3 studies. The Applicant assumed that the ratio of the effect expressed as the difference
between the on-demand treatment versus emicizumab in target joint bleeds over that in joint
bleeds would be applicable to characterize the marstacimab effect of the same endpoints. The
effect in target joint bleeds is consistently 49% of that in joint bleeds. Therefore, the
noninferiority margin of 1.2, derived as 49% of the margin of 2.5 for the joint bleeds, was
selected for target joint bleeds, expecting that a similar magnitude (~84%) of the effect would be
preserved.

In the Applicant’s derivation of NI margin for target joint bleeds endpoint, both inhibitor and
noninhibitor populations were used to provide a more stable estimate of the ratio. If only
noninhibitor participants were considered (HAVEN 3), there would have no change in NI margin
for target joint bleeds.
Noninferiority Margin for Key Secondary Endpoint (ABR for Total Bleeds)
The incidence of target joint bleeds was not an efficacy endpoint in any of the three hemophilia
studies in which the estimates for several bleed count endpoints were derived. In the absence of
internal data, the Applicant relied on the results of emicizumab publications of HAVEN1 and
HAVEN3 studies. The Applicant assumed that the ratio of the effect expressed as the difference
between the on-demand treatment versus emicizumab in total bleeds over that in treated bleeds
would be applicable to characterize the marstacimab effect of the same endpoints. The effect in
total bleeds exceeds 12% to 23% that in treated bleeds, resulting in the geometric mean of a 17%
increase. Therefore, the noninferiority margin of 2.9, derived as 117% of the margin of 2.5 for
188

Reference ID: 5461669

BLA 761369
HYMPAVZI (marstacimab-hncq) injection
the treated bleeds, was selected for total bleeds expecting that a similar magnitude (~85%) of the
effect would be preserved.

Source: Study B7841005 SAP v6 dated January 31, 2023, Appendix Table A4.2.1.4

In the Applicant’s derivation of NI margin for total bleeds endpoint, both inhibitor and
noninhibitor populations were used to provide a more stable estimate of the ratio. However, if

higher (3.1) as derived as 123% of the margin 2.5.

16.4. Results for Superiority Over Factor
Prophylaxis
As noted in Section 6.2.1.4 with the discussion of hierarchical testing and multiple testing
strategies, the review team considered Study B7841005 noninhibitor cohorts as the equivalent of
independent substudies, and individual alpha (0.05) could be tested to each cohort for superiority
versus on-demand factor and noninferiority/superiority versus factor prophylaxis separately. The
results of the analysis of superiority versus factor prophylaxis did not contribute to the Agency’s
regulatory decision and are therefore not included in Section 6. The results of the superiority
over factor prophylaxis analysis are presented here for completeness.
Table 105 presents the results for parametric and nonparametric analyses for the primary (ABR
treated bleeds) and key secondary ABR endpoints. For the primary endpoint, the Wilcoxon
signed rank test had a large p-value of 0.0788 (versus 0.0376 based on the parametric method,
that is, the negative binomial regression model). Similarly, for the secondary endpoint ABR of
total bleeds, the Wilcoxon signed rank test had a large p-value of 0.0573 (versus 0.0406 based on
the negative binomial regression model). The results for the treated bleeds and total bleeds
showed nominally statistical significance for superiority over routine prophylaxis but not in the
results based on the Wilcoxon signed rank test. In addition, the small nominal p-values for the
treated bleeds and total bleeds may be due to extreme high ABRs during the OP in the HA
population. The results for other types of bleeds (spontaneous bleeds, joint bleeds, and target
joint bleeds) were nominally not statistically significant for superiority over routine prophylaxis
regardless of the parametric and nonparametric methods were used.
The review team acknowledged that the nonparametric test could be less powerful and thus had
larger p-values. However, overall, the results were determined not to be robust because
parametric and nonparametric methods reached different conclusions. Therefore, the superiority
over routine prophylaxis could not be claimed.

2 P-value based on Wilcoxon signed rank test for the null hypothesis that the median ABR difference=2.5 for noninferiority
3 P-value based on Wilcoxon signed rank test for the null hypothesis that the median ABR difference=0 for superiority

16.5. Sensitivity Analysis for Extreme Values
Figure 50 shows the distribution of ABRs for participants in cohort OP-PPX by hemophilia type.
For HA and HB, the distribution is shown for OP (lower graph) and ATP (upper graph). As seen
in the figure, the distributions of the primary endpoint (ABR for treated bleeds) for HA and HB
populations are different. The range of ABRs is wider in HA participants compared to HB
participants. This would be expected as the HA phenotype is generally more severe than HB
phenotype, and so higher ABRs would be expected both in the OP and the ATP. However, in
Study B7841005, for the HA population, the distribution tail, with several extreme values, is
longer for the OP compared to the ATP. In contrast in the HB population, the tail, with several
extreme values, is longer in ATP compared to OP.
When there are extreme values in the ATP not seen in the OP, as with the HB population, the
mean difference of the ABR is pushed higher, suggesting poorer bleeding control with
marstacimab prophylaxis compared to factor prophylaxis. This is discussed as a key efficacy
issue in Section 6.3.1. When there are extreme values in the OP not seen the ATP, as with the
HA population, the mean difference of the ABR is pushed lower, suggesting poorer bleed control
with factor prophylaxis compared to marstacimab prophylaxis. This can be due to poor
compliance with the OP or chance.
To assess the robustness of the final results and assay sensitivity for extreme values (ABR> 40
for HA, ABR > 10 for HB), the Applicant, at the request of the Agency, conducted additional
sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint using the Negative Binomial Regression Model and
Wilcoxon Signed Rank test, using different imputation methods (mean, median, and zero ABR
imputation) for extreme ABRs. Specifically, imputations were requested using different cutoffs
to determine extreme ABRs considering HA and HB separately and HA and HB together. The
Applicant conducted the requested analyses by imputing the mean (or median) from the
remaining (non-imputed) participants based on the pooled HA and HB populations.
According to the Applicant’s analysis results, non-inferiority vs factor prophylaxis was met for
all imputation methods for all scenarios. However, the review team determined this approach,
which pools HA and HB ABRs in calculating the imputed value, may not be appropriate given
the differences in the ranges of the ABR distributions between HA and HB. Thus, the review
team conducted additional, more conservative, sensitivity analyses by replacing the extreme
ABRs by mean (or median) of the ABRs of remaining participants with the corresponding
hemophilia A (or B) type. That is, extreme HA ABRs are replaced by the mean (median) ABR of
the remaining HA participants and HB ABRs are replaced by the mean (median) ABR of the
remaining HB participants. Non-inferiority versus factor prophylaxis was also met for all
scenarios using these imputation methods.
In conclusion, these additional sensitivity analyses using parametric and non-parametric analyses
with different imputation methods for extreme ABRs in HA and HB populations supported the
final results for noninferiority versus factor prophylaxis for the primary endpoint (ABR for
treated bleeds).

17. Clinical Safety
17.1. Assessment of Safety by Observation Period
Treatment (Factor On-Demand, Factor
Prophylaxis)
In Section 6.3, the safety analysis pooled the OP-OD and OP-PPX cohorts. This was done
because both cohorts received factor replacement during OP with the only difference being
frequency of administration (on-demand versus prophylaxis) and all participants, regardless of
observational phase treatment, received the same ATP marstacimab regimen; therefore, the
treatment received prior to enrollment and during OP is not expected to impact the safety profile
of marstacimab overall.
The demographics and baseline disease characteristics are shown in Table 106. There are
differences in the age distribution between the two cohorts. A higher percentage of participants
were <18 years of age in the OP-PPX cohort (21%) compared to the OP-OD cohort (6%);
however, a higher percentage of participants in the OP-OD cohort were 18 to 44 years of age
(82%) compared to the OP-PPX cohort (52%). There are differences in site enrollment between
the two cohorts, most notably, a higher percentage of participants in the OP-OD cohort were
enrolled in Asia (64%) compared to the OP-PPX cohort (36%). This likely reflects standard
treatment practices (on-demand versus prophylaxis use of factor) in different regions. There are
some differences in number of baseline number of target joints, notably, a higher proportion of
participants with ≥3 target joints in the OP-OD cohort (36%) compared to OP-PPX (16%). This
may reflect effectiveness of factor prophylaxis compared to factor OD.
Table 107 provides an overview of frequency analysis of safety during the ATP with
marstacimab in Study B7841005, by cohort. The overall rate of treatment-emergent adverse
events (TEAEs) was higher in the OP-PPX (75%), compared to the OP-OD; however, this is
primarily accounted for by an increase in mild events in the OP-PPX. While all SAEs occurred
in the OP-PPX, as noted in Section 7.6.1.3, all but one of the SAEs reported in the primary safety
population during the ATP for Study 7684015 were assessed as not related to marstacimab.
The difference in frequency for TEAEs based on FMQ (narrow) (Table 108) between cohorts
was <10% for all FMQs (narrow) with the exception of viral infection, which occurred in 27% of
participants in the OP-PPX cohort compared to 6% in the OP-OD cohort, and hemorrhage, which
occurred in 13% of participants in the OP-PPX cohort compared to 3% in the OP-OD cohort.
The higher frequency of viral infections in the OP-PPX cohort may reflect a slightly older
population or may be due to chance, but based on mechanism of action is unlikely to be due to
marstacimab. The event of hemorrhage is related to underlying disease.
Based on a comparison of safety profiles in the OP-OD and OP-PPX cohorts, the review team
concluded that there were no clinically meaningful differences and reporting in the United States
Prescribing Information (USPI) by prior factor therapy (i.e., by cohort) was not needed to
support the safe and effective use of marstacimab.

17.2. Assessment of Safety by Hemophilia Type
In Section 6.3, the primary safety population includes participants with HA and HB. Based on
the similarity of the underlying mechanism of HA and HB and the mechanism of action of
marstacimab, Study B7841005does not enroll participants HA and HB to separate cohorts. The
safety profile is not expected to be different in these two subpopulations.
Table 109 provides a frequency-based overview of TEAEs by hemophilia type reported in the
safety population during the active treatment phase for Study B7841005. Overall, the analysis of
TEAEs by hemophilia type showed no significant differences in the frequency of adverse events
between the two groups (HA versus HB). As noted above, only one SAE was assessed as related
to marstacimab, and therefore, analysis of SAEs by subgroup is not meaningful. Table 110
presents the most common TEAEs by system organ class (SOC) and preferred term (PT) and
hemophilia type. There are no significant differences in frequency for any specific PT or SOC
between HA and HB.
Based on this subgroup analysis, the review team concludes that reporting in the USPI by
hemophilia subtype not needed for the safe and effective use of marstacimab.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
A clinical inspection summary for BLA 761369 was completed by the Division of Clinical
Compliance Evaluation in the Office of Scientific Investigations with a final signature date of
August 1, 2024. Four clinical sites were inspected for Study B7841005 (Site 1123 [Nirmal
Kumar Choraria, MD (Surat, Gujarat India)], Site 1035 [Young Shil Park, MD (Seoul, South
Korea), Site 1127 [Javier Morales Adrian, MD (Merida, Yucatan, Mexico) and Site 1013
[Laurent Frenzel, MD (Paris,France)]).
The Office of Scientific Investigations concluded that, based on the inspections, the study data
derived from these four clinical sites are considered reliable and are acceptable. The study data
submitted to the Agency for assessment appears acceptable in support of the proposed indication.
Protocol Deviations
Study B7841005
The Application provided a listing of protocol deviations. A summary of deviations classified as
“important protocol deviations” reported is presented in Table 111. Of note, dosing
administration error occurred in 20% of participants across the cohorts (n=22). This occurred
only once in each of the participants and were assessed as having no impact on overall efficacy
or safety findings of the study.
Study Site 1053 (Monterrey, Mexico) was terminated by the Applicant on March 2022, due to
significant noncompliance of Good Clinical Practice (GCP) leading to missing and unverified
data noted during the conduct of the study. This site screened a total of four

participants: three participants failed screening and 1 adolescent participant was administered
marstacimab but discontinued from treatment when study conduct at this site was terminated. No
deaths were reported. All data from participants at this site were maintained in the clinical
database but excluded from the clinical study report data analysis.

23. Labeling: Key Changes and Considerations
This United States Prescribing Information (USPI) review includes a high-level summary of the
rationale for major changes to the finalized USPI as compared to the Applicant’s draft USPI
(Table 112). The USPI was reviewed to ensure that USPI meets regulatory/statutory
requirements, is consistent with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and
scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides
clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.
